# Principles List

Merged psychology, color, habit, HCI, accessibility, AI-interface and ethical-design reference for web apps, SaaS and mobile apps.

**Merge date:** 15 November 2026. **Basis:** six uploaded files plus the substantive summary in the request (S7). This is a merge of supplied material, not a claim to catalogue every psychological principle or independently validate every 2026 research/legal claim.

**Catalogue:** 283 numbered entries. Entries include principles, frameworks, subtopic coverage, design patterns, caveats and playbooks; this count is not a count of distinct experimentally established laws. Matching labels are grouped; related but different mechanisms remain separate.

**Completeness:** every uploaded file is preserved verbatim in Appendix B, including its examples, tables as received, figures, limitations, bibliography, and conflicting statements. The main list groups the usable material; Appendix B protects supporting logic that a concise list could otherwise lose. Repeated source wording there is intentional and is not counted again.

**Unavailable material:** the summaries mention a 198-item downloadable catalogue, a full habit catalogue, 40 HEX palettes and 60 research links. Their linked target files are not present among these uploads. Only supplied content can be merged; promised-but-absent details are identified, not invented.

**Reading rule:** the corrections below take precedence over conflicting source wording. Attributed text preserves source logic and does not independently endorse every empirical claim, numeric recommendation or legal statement. Clinical addiction is not interchangeable with engaging or habitual use.
## Accuracy corrections and interpretation rules

1. **Memory capacity is not a menu quota.** Neither 7±2 nor roughly four chunks justifies a universal maximum for menu items, CTAs, onboarding steps, or comparison columns. Task, chunking, expertise and external memory aids matter. The original numeric UI prescriptions are retained only as source wording.
2. **Heuristics are not universal laws.** Pareto 80/20, Parkinson, 60/30/10, three tiers, F/Z scanning, thumb-zone maps, the 400 ms Doherty threshold and 0.1/1/10-second response-time guidance are contextual heuristics. Do not promise their numeric thresholds or layouts will work universally.
3. **Choice, loss, framing and social influence are conditional.** Choice overload, loss aversion, decoys, defaults, social proof, authority and scarcity vary with task and population. Neither a near-2 meta-analytic loss coefficient nor a famous jam-study result is a universal conversion multiplier. A purchasable decoy or truthful premise alone does not make every deployment fair.
4. **Habit, reinforcement and addiction are distinct.** Habitual use, engagement, problematic use and clinical addiction differ. Dopamine is not a synonym for pleasure or addiction. Variable reinforcement does not establish addiction; feeds are not automatically formal variable-ratio schedules. Positive/negative describe adding/removing consequences; reinforcement/punishment describe increasing/decreasing behaviour. A badge removal is negative reinforcement only if it actually strengthens the preceding action.
5. **Framework labels do not establish scientific status.** Fogg, Hook, PSYCH, REBEL and commercial playbooks should retain their provenance and limits. B=MAP is a conceptual convergence model, not literal multiplication or addition. Do not assert activation failure is almost always an ability problem or that rewards invariably habituate within days.
6. **Color associations do not guarantee behaviour.** No supplied evidence establishes an addictive hue, universally best CTA color, endocrine trust switch, or deterministic personality-to-palette mapping. Red badges do not inherently bypass executive control. Distinguish visual association, subjective rating, observed behaviour and causal physiological evidence.
7. **Color models and contrast must not be conflated.** Hue, chroma/saturation, luminance, lightness and brightness are not interchangeable. Complementary hues do not guarantee luminance contrast. RGB is display mixing; RYB is a traditional art model; cone responses overlap. Pure-black avoidance and reduced saturation are options to test, not universal medical rules.
8. **WCAG 2.2 thresholds have scope and exceptions.** Normal text generally requires 4.5:1 and large text 3:1 under SC 1.4.3; applicable non-text controls/graphics need 3:1 under SC 1.4.11. Color alone must not carry meaning under SC 1.4.1. These criteria have definitions and exceptions; passing a few pairs is not full conformance. [W3C WCAG 2.2](https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/).
9. **Target size is not an unconditional legal floor.** SC 2.5.8 uses a 24×24 CSS-pixel AA target-size requirement with spacing, equivalent-control, inline, user-agent and essential exceptions. SC 2.5.5 uses 44×44 CSS pixels at AAA, also with exceptions. CSS px, points and dp are different units. A standard is not automatically law in every jurisdiction. [W3C target-size explanation](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/target-size-minimum.html).
10. **APCA figures remain source-reported supplementary guidance.** The uploads conflict about APCA adoption and status. Their Lc table and suggested levels are preserved, not independently validated here. APCA checks do not demonstrate WCAG 2.2 contrast compliance; distinguish a draft, recommendation, algorithm version and conformance requirement.
11. **AI confidence and explanation need validation.** A model-generated percentage is not automatically calibrated confidence; a citation can be wrong, and an explanation does not automatically produce appropriate reliance. A readable evidence-based rationale is different from access to hidden model reasoning. Match confirmation and oversight to risk and meaningful user control.
12. **AI and emotion claims are not established standards.** The supplied 18 Human–AI Interaction guidelines are a CHI 2019 framework, not a new 2026 law. The official paper reports evaluation with 49 practitioners across 20 products. Tap pressure, scroll speed or hesitation do not establish reliable emotion detection; streaming, disclosure and humanlike personas have context-dependent effects. [Microsoft Research](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/guidelines-for-human-ai-interaction/).
13. **Legal snapshots and 2026 findings are not reverified by merging.** Retain descriptions of the DSA, Digital Fairness Act proposal, FTC click-to-cancel, ROSCA, AI Act, CPRA, ICO guidance, TikTok preliminary findings, settlements and dates as source-reported material. Proposals, preliminary findings, enacted rules and final judgments are different. Do not rely on these passages as a current compliance checklist.
14. **Population and evidence quality remain attached to numbers.** Color-vision prevalence differs by sex, ancestry, region and sampling. Study sample sizes, percentages, coefficients, dates and effect estimates below are source-reported unless specifically checked above. Correlation, neural activation and laboratory effects do not alone establish real-world causal harm.
15. **Unresolved references remain unresolved.** Historical citation tokens such as turn… identifiers and sandbox download links are preserved in the source record, but they cannot be verified or turned into real citations from the available text alone. Existing author names and URLs are retained; none were fabricated.
16. **Memory and motivational folklore require qualifications.** Zeigarnik memory advantage and interrupted-task resumption are distinct. Habit formation has no fixed 21/66-day deadline; there is no validated universal eight-second attention span or three-click maximum. Aesthetics, gamification, reassurance and excellent recovery do not guarantee usability, motivation or loyalty.

## Numbered principles list

### Information processing and cognitive capacity

1. **Working memory and Miller's Law**

   *Source: S1, line 19.*

   Working memory is limited. Miller (1956) said about 7 plus or minus 2 items; Cowan (2001) revised it to about 4 chunks. Apply: chunk data, split long forms into steps, format phone, card and license numbers in groups, keep context visible so users never carry info between screens, test grouping and external memory aids; do not infer a four-item interface quota. Watch: it is not a rule that menus need 7 items (see Section 17).

   *Source: S2, 1.11.*

   - **What:** The average person can hold ~7±2 items in working memory *(Miller, 1956)* — modern research revises this down to about 4 *(Cowan, 2001)*.
   - **Apply:** Limit menus to ~5–7 top-level items; chunk longer lists; never require users to remember info from one screen to another (keep context visible).

   *Source: S2, 4.1.*

   - **What:** Modern estimates put working memory at about 4±1 chunks, not 7 *(Cowan, 2001)*.
   - **Apply:** Design for 4: max 4 top actions, 4 onboarding steps per screen, 4 items per comparison row. Cross-reference Miller's Law (1.11) — plan for the stricter number.

2. **Chunking**

   *Source: S1, line 20.*

   Meaningful groups are held better than raw items. Apply: group settings into categories, break onboarding into 3 to 4 steps, group dashboard metrics logically (unfiltered dozens of metrics break working memory).

   *Source: S2, 1.3.*

   - **What:** People hold information better when it is grouped into meaningful chunks *(rooted in Miller's 7±2)*.
   - **Apply:** Break long forms into steps; group settings into categories; format phone numbers, card numbers, and dates; chunk onboarding into 3–4 steps.

3. **Cognitive Load Theory (Sweller)**

   *Source: S1, line 21.*

   Effort splits into three kinds.
     - **Intrinsic load**: inherent task complexity. Cannot be removed, only managed with progressive, bite-sized steps.
     - **Extraneous load**: effort wasted decoding a poor interface (clutter, inconsistent patterns, jargon, misalignment). The main UX target to cut.
     - **Germane load**: effort that builds lasting mental schemas. Free it up by removing noise; support with intuitive onboarding and consistent interactions.
     - Apply: one primary action per screen, hide advanced options, use defaults, cut jargon, ask for config at the moment of need. Watch: load includes visual noise, dense text and decision count, not only clicks.

   *Source: S2, 1.4.*

   - **What:** Working memory has limited capacity; every element that must be parsed competes for it *(Sweller's Cognitive Load Theory, 1988)*.
   - **Apply:** One primary action per screen; hide advanced options; use defaults; cut jargon; defer configuration to context ("ask at the moment of need").
   - **Watch:** "Load" includes visual noise, dense text, and decision count — not just clicks.

4. **Selective attention**

   *Source: S1, line 26.*

   The brain filters to goal-relevant stimuli. Apply: use contrast and placement to steer focus to primary CTAs, never rely on a small banner being noticed, use multiple channels for critical info, put what must be seen in the task path.

   *Source: S2, 1.22.*

   - **What:** People attend to a subset of the environment and miss the rest, even prominent stimuli *(cocktail party effect; Cherry, 1953)*.
   - **Apply:** Never rely on users noticing a small banner; use multiple channels (visual + email + push) for critical info; put what you need seen *in the task path*, not beside it.

5. **Paradox of the Active User (Carroll and Rosson, 1987)**

   *Source: S1, line 27.*

   Users skip manuals and start doing. Apply: self-evident first task, contextual teaching at the moment of need, empty states and inline tips instead of tours, skip-and-succeed paths. Watch: forced long tours are read by almost nobody.

   *Source: S2, 1.21.*

   - **What:** Users immediately start trying to *do* things rather than reading docs or tutorials — they learn by doing *(Carroll & Rosson, 1987)*.
   - **Apply:** No mandatory manuals: make the first task self-evident, teach features contextually at the moment of need, use empty states and inline tips instead of tours.
   - **Watch:** Long forced onboarding tours are read by almost nobody — design for skip-and-succeed.

6. **Progressive disclosure**

   *Source: S1, line 28.*

   Show only what is needed now, defer the rest. Apply: accordions, advanced settings, wizards, context-revealed features. Most reliable fix for cognitive load in SaaS.

   *Source: S2, 1.24.*

   - **What:** Show only what's needed now; defer the rest until requested or relevant.
   - **Apply:** Accordions, "advanced settings," step-by-step wizards, context-revealed features. The single most reliable technique against cognitive load in SaaS.

7. **Recognition over recall**

   *Source: S1, line 29.*

   Recognizing is easier than remembering. Apply: icons with labels, recently used items, search suggestions, breadcrumbs, persistent navigation, autofill. Never ask users to remember an ID, code or prior screen.

   *Source: S2, 1.25.*

   - **What:** Recognizing something seen before is far easier than recalling it from memory (a core Nielsen heuristic).
   - **Apply:** Icons + labels (not icons alone), recently used items, search suggestions, breadcrumbs, persistent navigation, autofill; never ask users to remember an ID, code, or prior screen.

8. **Signal-to-noise ratio**

   *Source: S1, line 30.*

   Relevant info divided by irrelevant info. Apply: cut decoration from task-critical screens; noise includes badge spam, alert inflation, marketing inserts in workflows.

   *Source: S2, 1.26.*

   - **What:** Relevant information divided by irrelevant information on a display; higher ratio = easier processing.
   - **Apply:** Cut decorative elements from task-critical screens; every pixel of ornament must earn the attention it consumes. Noise includes badge spam, alert inflation, and marketing inserts inside workflows.

9. **Dual-process theory (System 1 and System 2, Kahneman and Tversky)**

   *Source: S1, line 31.*

   System 1 is fast, automatic, heuristic. System 2 is slow, analytical, tiring. Apply: design routine paths for System 1; add deliberate friction where System 2 should engage (money, deletion, security). Watch: keeping users in System 1 on purpose to block reflection is manipulation.

10. **Dynamic friction**

   *Source: S1, line 32.*

   Algorithmically adding or removing cognitive load based on the user's trajectory (skip tutorials for experts, simplify checkout on hesitation). Watch: the same mechanism can make the platform's preferred path always the easiest.

11. **Information bias**

   *Source: S1, line 33.*

   People overvalue extra information even when it changes nothing. Apply: fewer decision-relevant dashboard metrics, detail on demand.

   *Source: S2, 5.21.*

   - **What:** We over-value more information even when it changes nothing.
   - **Apply:** In dashboards, curate: fewer, decision-relevant metrics beat exhaustive feeds; offer "more detail" on demand rather than by default.

### Decision dynamics and choice architecture

12. **Hick's Law (Hick-Hyman)**

   *Source: S1, line 37.*

   Decision time grows logarithmically with number and complexity of choices: T = a + b * log2(n + 1). Apply: fewer options per screen, progressive disclosure, search over mega-menus, one decision per screen, limited nav items, simpler pricing matrices. Watch: cutting too hard can starve power users (Postel's Law, Tesler's Law).

   *Source: S2, 1.9.*

   - **What:** Decision time increases logarithmically with the number and complexity of choices *(Hick & Hyman, 1952)*.
   - **Apply:** Reduce options per screen; progressive disclosure; smart search over mega-menus; break long processes into screens with one decision each.
   - **Watch:** Cutting options entirely can violate Postel's Law (1.16) — power users need depth somewhere.

13. **Choice overload (Paradox of Choice)**

   *Source: S1, line 38.*

   Large option sets can lower conversion or satisfaction in some contexts. Iyengar and Lepper jam study: 24 jams = 3% purchase, 6 jams = 30%. Apply: 3 pricing tiers, 6 to 9 nav items, "Recommended" defaults, progressive filters; single-option recommendation in onboarding is reported to cut friction by up to 24%. Watch: not universal; effect size depends on task and domain (Section 17).

   *Source: S2, 1.2.*

   - **What:** More options raise decision effort and lower conversion and satisfaction *(Iyengar & Lepper's jam study, 2000: 24 jams → 3% purchase, 6 jams → 30%)*.
   - **Apply:** Cap visible choices (3 pricing tiers, 6–9 nav items), use "recommended/Popular" defaults, progressive filters, and smart defaults instead of exhaustive lists.
   - **Watch:** False scarcity of options ("only 3 left!") crosses into dark-pattern territory (§14).

14. **Pareto Principle (80/20)**

   *Source: S1, line 39.*

   The 80/20 feature-use split is a prioritization heuristic to validate with product data. Apply: make the core 20% frictionless, nest edge-case features, fix the top few errors users actually hit.

   *Source: S2, 1.13.*

   - **What:** ~80% of effects come from ~20% of causes; in software, most users use a small fraction of features.
   - **Apply:** Surface the critical 20% of features; bury or cut the rest; prioritize fixing the top few error cases users actually hit.

15. **Occam's Razor**

   *Source: S1, line 40.*

   Prefer the solution with the fewest steps and assumptions. Apply: remove fields, steps and options that do not earn their place.

   *Source: S2, 1.12.*

   - **What:** Among competing explanations, the simplest should be selected; by extension, avoid unnecessary complexity in solutions.
   - **Apply:** Remove steps, fields, and options that don't earn their place; every element must justify the attention it costs.

16. **Parkinson's Law**

   *Source: S1, line 41.*

   Work expands to fill available time. Apply: show short completion times ("two minutes"), default to short estimates, time-boxing affordances.

   *Source: S2, 1.14.*

   - **What:** Work expands to fill the time available for it.
   - **Apply:** In productivity/task apps, default to short time estimates and let users extend; use "planned duration" nudges; show time-boxing affordances (Pomodoro-style).

17. **Confirmation bias**

   *Source: S1, line 42.*

   People favor info that fits existing beliefs. Apply: keep brand promise consistent from marketing site to dashboard; in analytics, show disconfirming evidence; in onboarding, surface the missing step rather than only validating the plan.

   *Source: S2, 5.9.*

   - **What:** We seek and favor information that confirms existing beliefs.
   - **Apply:** In analytics/dashboards, present disconfirming evidence proactively (show the segment where the campaign failed); in onboarding, don't just validate the user's plan — surface the missing step.

18. **Priming**

   *Source: S1, line 43.*

   Earlier stimuli shape later responses. Apply: premium visual quality at the marketing stage primes trust in the dashboard; use context-consistent imagery and copy. Watch: many priming studies failed replication; do not claim causality you cannot measure.

   *Source: S2, 3.13.*

   - **What:** Prior stimuli unconsciously shape subsequent responses (e.g., prices, imagery) — with the major caveat that many priming studies failed replication.
   - **Apply (safe form):** Context-consistent imagery and copy (success imagery before an upgrade page); avoid implying causality you can't measure.

19. **Anchoring**

   *Source: S1, line 44.*

   The first number seen skews later judgments (Tversky and Kahneman, 1974). Apply: premium tier first or on the left, original price next to discount, "from $X". Watch: fake "was" prices are deceptive pricing.

   *Source: S2, 5.1.*

   - **What:** The first number seen skews all later judgments *(Tversky & Kahneman, 1974)*.
   - **Apply:** Show the original price next to the discount; present the premium plan first on pricing pages; anchor with "from $X" before itemized features.
   - **Watch:** Fake reference prices (inflated "was" prices) are regulated as deceptive pricing in many jurisdictions.

20. **Compromise effect (extremeness aversion)**

   *Source: S1, line 45.*

   People avoid the cheapest and most expensive and pick the middle. Apply: pair a high anchor with a highlighted middle tier.

21. **Decoy effect (asymmetric dominance, Huber, Payne and Puto, 1982)**

   *Source: S1, line 46.*

   A clearly inferior third option shifts choice toward the target. Example: $10 basic, $20 pro, $22 pro-plus. Apply: decoys must be genuinely purchasable, not imaginary.

   *Source: S2, 5.8.*

   - **What:** Adding a third, clearly inferior option shifts choice toward the target *(Huber, Payne & Puto, 1982)*.
   - **Apply:** Three-tier pricing where the middle plan is designed to dominate the decoy (similar price, more value) and be beaten only by the premium; decoys must be genuinely purchasable, not imaginary.

22. **Availability heuristic**

   *Source: S1, line 47.*

   Likelihood is judged by how easily examples come to mind. Apply: a recent outage outweighs months of uptime, so communicate reliability proactively (status page, uptime badge).

   *Source: S2, 5.2.*

   - **What:** We judge likelihood by how easily examples come to mind.
   - **Apply:** Recent, vivid events dominate perception — a recent outage outweighs months of uptime. Communicate reliability proactively (status pages, uptime badges) rather than letting the last incident define you.

23. **Affect heuristic**

   *Source: S1, line 48.*

   Decisions ride on current feeling. Apply: playful tone for low stakes, calm data-forward tone for money, security and deletion.

   *Source: S2, 5.3.*

   - **What:** Decisions ride on current feelings rather than analysis; positive affect shrinks perceived risk.
   - **Apply:** Match emotional tone to decision stakes: playful tone for low-stakes choices; calm, data-forward tone for money, security, and deletion flows.

24. **Framing effect**

   *Source: S1, line 49.*

   Same facts, different presentation, different choice ("90% success" vs "10% failure"). Apply: frame benefits positively by default; use loss framing only with honest stakes. Watch: effect size depends on context (Section 17).

   *Source: S2, 5.4.*

   - **What:** Logically identical options are judged differently by presentation: "90% success" vs "10% failure"; "save $50" vs "lose $50."
   - **Apply:** Frame benefits positively by default ("keep your data" vs "don't lose your data"); for loss-framing (more motivating), use honestly: "You'll lose your streak if you skip today."

25. **Loss aversion**

   *Source: S1, line 50.*

   Losses weigh more than equivalent gains. Classic estimate is about 2x; a 2024 meta-analysis (607 estimates, 150 papers) found a mean near 1.96 with real heterogeneity. Apply: trials framed as what you will lose when they end (list of locked features), show what users already have at stake before downgrade prompts, streak and expiring-credit language. Watch: manufactured losses and punitive recovery are coercion; loss aversion powers most dark patterns.

   *Source: S2, 5.5.*

   - **What:** Losses weigh roughly twice as much as equivalent gains *(Kahneman & Tversky, prospect theory, 1979)*.
   - **Apply:** Free trials convert better framed as "what you'll lose when it ends" (list of locked features) than as "what you'll gain"; show what users already have at stake (data, setups, streaks) before plan downgrade prompts.
   - **Watch:** Loss aversion is the engine of most dark patterns (fake countdowns, fear-based copy). Use real stakes only.

26. **Status quo bias**

   *Source: S1, line 51.*

   Inertia makes the current state the default path (Samuelson and Zeckhauser, 1988). Apply: make the desired behavior the status quo (sensible preselections, annual billing with monthly toggle); keep delete and downgrade deliberately considered.

   *Source: S2, 5.6.*

   - **What:** Inertia: the current state feels safest and is the default path *(Samuelson & Zeckhauser, 1988)*.
   - **Apply:** Design the desired behavior *as* the status quo: auto-enroll in annual billing with a monthly toggle, preselect sensible settings, keep good flows frictionless and bad flows (delete, downgrade) deliberately considered.

27. **Default effect**

   *Source: S1, line 52.*

   Preselection can strongly influence choice (Johnson and Goldstein, 2003; Thaler and Sunstein, 2008). Apply: smart, visible, changeable defaults for notifications, plan, templates, privacy. Watch: defaults that serve the business against the user (opt-out data sharing) are manipulative and increasingly illegal.

   *Source: S2, 5.7.*

   - **What:** Preselection can strongly influence choice overwhelmingly (organ-donation defaults; Johnson & Goldstein, 2003; "Nudge," Thaler & Sunstein, 2008).
   - **Apply:** Smart defaults are your highest-leverage decision tool: default notification settings, plan selection, template choice, privacy levels (that still protect the user). Always visible + changeable.
   - **Watch:** Defaults that serve the business against user interest (opt-out data sharing) are the definition of a manipulative nudge — and increasingly illegal (§14).

28. **Negativity bias**

   *Source: S1, line 53.*

   Negatives weigh more than positives (Rozin and Royzman, 2001). Apply: surface review volume and response quality, lead error states with the fix, moderate communities.

   *Source: S2, 5.10.*

   - **What:** Negative events, reviews, and feedback have greater psychological impact than positive ones *(Rozin & Royzman, 2001)*.
   - **Apply:** One 3-star review outweighs ten 5-star ones in perception — surface review *volume* and response quality; in error states, lead with the fix, not the fault; moderate communities actively.

29. **Optimism bias**

   *Source: S1, line 54.*

   People overestimate future behavior ("I'll use this daily"). Apply: design re-engagement journeys for annual plans and courses.

   *Source: S2, 5.11.*

   - **What:** People systematically overestimate their future behavior: "I'll definitely use this daily," "I'll finish this course."
   - **Apply:** Annual plans, course purchases, and gym-model subscriptions exploit optimism honestly by designing *for* the return (re-engagement journeys), not just the purchase. In planning tools, counter with estimates from similar past projects.

30. **Planning fallacy**

   *Source: S1, line 55.*

   People underestimate task duration (Kahneman and Tversky, 1979). Apply: prefill estimates from history, show buffers, forecast from actual velocity.

   *Source: S2, 5.12.*

   - **What:** People underestimate task duration even when they know past tasks ran late *(Kahneman & Tversky, 1979; Buehler et al., 1994)*.
   - **Apply:** In project/task tools, prefill estimates from historical data; show buffer time; progress forecasts based on actual velocity, not stated plans.

31. **Projection bias**

   *Source: S1, line 56.*

   People project their calm present state onto their future self and assume they will resist temptation. Apply: offer commitment tools, do not rely on future willpower.

32. **Present bias (hyperbolic discounting, Laibson beta)**

   *Source: S1, line 57.*

   Value drops steeply between now and soon, flatly later; this causes preference reversals. Apply: make future benefit and future cost concrete and near ("Card charged in 3 days, click to change"), deliver value in the first session. Watch: platforms compress time horizons (autoplay, micro-moments), place friction asymmetrically (easy to spend, hard to leave or cancel), and use free trials where the benefit is vivid and the cost abstract.

   *Source: S2, 5.13.*

   - **What:** Rewards now are vastly overweighted vs rewards later; costs later are underweighted.
   - **Apply:** Make future rewards concrete and near ("Save 4 hours *this week*"); make future costs concrete and near ("Card charged in 3 days, click to change"); deliver immediate value in the first session — never "value in 3 weeks."

33. **Sunk cost fallacy**

   *Source: S1, line 58.*

   Past investment increases commitment (Arkes and Blumer, 1985). Apply: surface accumulated value honestly at renewal, offer one-click import to lower switching cost. Watch: trapping users in unwanted subscriptions is a roach motel.

   *Source: S2, 5.14.*

   - **What:** Past investment (time, money, data) irrationally increases commitment to continue *(Arkes & Blumer, 1985)*.
   - **Apply:** Honest use: surface accumulated value at renewal ("Your workspace holds 1,200 tasks and 3 years of history") — legitimate information. Migration tooling ("import everything in one click") lowers switching cost for the *user's* benefit.
   - **Watch:** Using sunk cost to trap users in unwanted subscriptions is a classic roach motel (§14).

34. **Opportunity cost neglect**

   *Source: S1, line 59.*

   Users ignore alternative uses of their time during an immediate digital reward. Watch: amplified by immersive interfaces.

35. **IKEA effect**

   *Source: S1, line 60.*

   Self-built things are overvalued (Norton, Mochon and Ariely, 2012). Apply: let users build their first dashboard, doc or workspace (prefilled but editable); user-made templates and automations raise switching cost legitimately.

   *Source: S2, 5.15.*

   - **What:** Products users partly create are valued far more *(Norton, Mochon & Ariely, 2012)*.
   - **Apply:** Co-creation moments in onboarding: let users build their first dashboard/doc/workspace (pre-filled but editable); user-created templates, themes, automations raise switching costs *because the user made them* — that's legitimate retention.

36. **Endowment effect**

   *Source: S1, line 61.*

   Ownership raises perceived value (Kahneman, Knetsch and Thaler, 1990). Apply: trials on real user data beat trials on demo data; "your workspace", "your reports". Conversion framed as not losing the workspace.

   *Source: S2, 5.16.*

   - **What:** Ownership raises valuation *(Kahneman, Knetsch & Thaler, 1990)*.
   - **Apply:** Trials that let users create real data (files, projects) convert better than trials on demo data; frame trial artifacts as "your workspace," "your reports."

37. **Bandwagon effect**

   *Source: S1, line 62.*

   People adopt what others adopt. Apply: "Join 40,000 teams", "most popular", with real current numbers.

   *Source: S2, 5.17.*

   - **What:** People adopt what others are adopting.
   - **Apply:** "Join 40,000 teams," trending templates, "most popular plan" badges — with real, current numbers.

38. **Survivorship bias**

   *Source: S1, line 63.*

   Visible winners hide invisible failures. Apply: show base rates and median outcomes; study churned users, not only power users.

   *Source: S2, 5.18.*

   - **What:** We learn from visible winners and ignore the invisible failures.
   - **Apply:** In case studies and benchmarks, show base rates ("median outcome across all customers"), not just stars; in your own analytics, examine churned users' journeys, not just power users'.

39. **Zero-risk bias**

   *Source: S1, line 64.*

   Eliminating a small risk entirely beats reducing a large one. Apply: money-back guarantee, "we never sell your data", zero-knowledge encryption.

   *Source: S2, 5.19.*

   - **What:** People prefer eliminating a small risk entirely over reducing a large one.
   - **Apply:** "Zero-knowledge" encryption, "we never sell your data" statements, 30-day money-back guarantees — removing one stated risk completely outperforms probabilistic reassurance.

40. **Ambiguity aversion (Ellsberg)**

   *Source: S1, line 65.*

   Known odds beat unknown odds. Apply: exact price before checkout, exact dates, plain "what happens after the trial".

   *Source: S2, 5.20.*

   - **What:** Known odds are preferred over unknown odds (Ellsberg paradox).
   - **Apply:** Remove ambiguity at decision points: exact price before checkout, exact delivery dates, "what happens after the trial" stated plainly. Hidden next-steps read as risk and stall conversion.

41. **Dunning-Kruger effect**

   *Source: S1, line 66.*

   Low skill inflates confidence. Apply: embed guidance invisibly (inline validation, auto-fixes); never gate basic flows behind "read the docs".

   *Source: S2, 5.22.*

   - **What:** Low skill inflates confidence; experts underestimate themselves *(Kruger & Dunning, 1999)*.
   - **Apply:** Beginners skip help because they overestimate competence — so embed guidance invisibly (inline validation, automatic fixes); never gate basic flows behind "read the docs first."

42. **Curse of knowledge**

   *Source: S1, line 67.*

   Experts cannot imagine not knowing. Apply: test copy with outsiders, label by user goals ("Send invoice") not internal architecture.

   *Source: S2, 5.23.*

   - **What:** Experts can't imagine not knowing what they know (Newton's tapper-listener study: ~2.5% of tunes guessed by listeners).
   - **Apply:** Feature naming, empty states, and error copy written by the team are unintelligible to new users; test copy with people outside the team; label features by *user goals*, not internal architecture ("Send invoice," not "Use AR module").

43. **Hindsight bias**

   *Source: S1, line 68.*

   Outcomes seem obvious afterward. Apply: compare post-mortems against logged forecasts.

   *Source: S2, 5.24.*

   - **What:** After an outcome, "it was obvious all along" — memory rewrites prediction.
   - **Apply:** In incident post-mortems and analytics reviews, compare against the *logged* forecast, not memory; in product retros, judge decisions with the information available then.

### Gestalt principles of visual perception

44. **Proximity**

   *Source: S1, line 72.*

   Near objects read as a group. Apply: label closer to its own input than to the previous one; whitespace often beats divider lines; space between cards greater than within cards.

   *Source: S2, 2.1.*

   - **What:** Objects near each other are perceived as a group.
   - **Apply:** Group a label with its input (not input with the neighboring label); space between cards > space within cards; related actions cluster together.

45. **Similarity**

   *Source: S1, line 73.*

   Shared color, shape, size or orientation signals same category. Apply: all primary actions share one style, all danger actions share one look, so users learn the platform's interaction grammar.

   *Source: S2, 2.2.*

   - **What:** Items sharing visual traits (color, size, shape) are perceived as related.
   - **Apply:** Consistent styling for all interactive elements; all "danger" actions share one look; visually distinguish but relate rows in a table.

46. **Common region**

   *Source: S1, line 74.*

   Elements inside one boundary form a cluster. Basis of the card pattern. Apply: cards, panels, background tints; put destructive actions outside the region holding safe ones.

   *Source: S2, 2.5.*

   - **What:** Elements inside the same enclosed area are perceived as grouped.
   - **Apply:** Cards, panels, and wells to group content; place destructive actions *outside* the region containing safe ones; use background tints to delimit sections.

47. **Closure**

   *Source: S1, line 75.*

   The mind completes incomplete shapes. Apply: minimal icons, partial borders, loading states, creative negative space. Watch: do not rely on closure for anything critical.

   *Source: S2, 2.3.*

   - **What:** The mind fills gaps to perceive complete forms.
   - **Apply:** Use outlines and partial borders for light-weight UI; minimal logos/icons still read correctly; don't rely on closure for anything critical to comprehension.

48. **Continuity**

   *Source: S1, line 76.*

   Elements on a line or smooth curve read as related. Apply: aligned table rows, linear wizards, swipe carousels, form alignment guiding order.

   *Source: S2, 2.4.*

   - **What:** Elements arranged on a line/curve are perceived as related and continuous.
   - **Apply:** Aligned rows/columns in data tables; stepper flows along one visual path; aligning form elements guides the eye through the intended order.

49. **Prägnanz (good figure)**

   *Source: S1, line 77.*

   Ambiguous stimuli are read in the simplest form. Apply: flat design, simple icons, generous negative space over skeuomorphic clutter; restructure charts that can be read two ways.

   *Source: S2, 2.7.*

   - **What:** Ambiguous stimuli are interpreted in the simplest form possible.
   - **Apply:** Simple, regular shapes read faster; avoid ornate icons that can be misread; when a chart can be read two ways, restructure it so only one reading is possible.

50. **Uniform connectedness**

   *Source: S1, line 78.*

   Connected elements (lines, arrows, shared bands) read as related, overriding proximity and similarity. Apply: workflow builders, multi-step trackers, data lineage graphs.

   *Source: S2, 2.8.*

   - **What:** Visually connected elements (lines, arrows, shared borders) are perceived as related.
   - **Apply:** Draw the flow: connect steps with arrows/progress lines; connect toggles to what they control with proximity + shared container.

51. **Figure-ground**

   *Source: S1, line 79.*

   Elements read as focus or background. Apply: modal with dimmed backdrop, elevated active card, enough contrast, no busy backgrounds behind text.

   *Source: S2, 2.6.*

   - **What:** We perceive elements as either figure (focus) or ground (background); ambiguous figures flip.
   - **Apply:** Modals with dimmed backdrop; elevate the active card; ensure sufficient contrast so the figure is never ambiguous; avoid busy backgrounds behind text.

52. **Synchrony (common fate)**

   *Source: S1, line 80.*

   Elements moving or changing together read as a group. Apply: shared animation timing for related cards; avoid accidental synchrony that implies false relationships.

   *Source: S2, 2.9.*

   - **What:** Elements moving together or changing at the same time are perceived as a group.
   - **Apply:** Group animations (cards animate in together = one group); shared transition timing communicates relatedness; avoid accidental synchronous animations that imply false relationships.

### Ergonomics, interaction physics and responsiveness

53. **Fitts's Law (1954)**

   *Source: S1, line 84.*

   Movement time MT = a + b * log2(1 + D/W); big, near targets are faster and less error-prone.
     - Desktop: context menus (distance near zero), screen edges and corners as "infinite" targets, adjacent related actions.
     - Mobile: screen edges do not help the thumb; primary actions go in the lower thumb zone.

   *Source: S2, 1.6.*

   - **What:** Time to acquire a target is a function of distance to and size of the target *(Fitts, 1954)*.
   - **Apply:** Large touch targets (≥44×44 pt iOS, ≥48×48 dp Android), place primary actions within the thumb zone (bottom of mobile screens), make edges/corners "infinitely large" targets, keep related actions adjacent.

54. **Touch target sizing**

   *Source: S1, line 87.*

   Finger pad is 10 to 14 mm. WCAG 2.5.8 AA minimum 24x24 CSS px (a conformance criterion with exceptions, not an unconditional legal or ergonomic floor). WCAG 2.5.5 AAA is 44x44 px, iOS 44x44 pt, Material 48x48 dp. Accuracy gains plateau past about 10 mm; oversized buttons waste space. Keep at least 8 dp between targets.

55. **Thumb zone and peripheral vision**

   *Source: S1, line 88.*

   Hoober field research found about 49% one-thumb use. Apply: primary actions and tab bars at the bottom, danger actions at the top, motion for periphery. Watch: universal thumb-zone diagrams are an overgeneralization (Section 17).

   *Source: S2, 3.8.*

   - **What:** Central (foveal) vision is sharp; peripheral is coarse but detects motion; on mobile, reachability maps to thumb arcs *(Steven Hoober's field research: ~49% one-thumb use)*.
   - **Apply:** Primary actions and tab bars at the bottom on mobile; danger actions at the top (hard to reach accidentally); motion/notifications for the periphery, text for the center.

56. **Spatial memory and gesture ergonomics**

   *Source: S1, line 89.*

   Standard swipes, double taps and long presses become muscle memory. Apply: avoid requiring reach to extreme top corners.

57. **Doherty threshold (1982 / 1988)**

   *Source: S1, line 90.*

   About 400 ms is a historical responsiveness heuristic, not a universal instant-perception threshold. Apply: optimistic UI, skeleton screens, prefetching, progress indicators beyond that, transitions of at least 100 ms so change is noticed. Watch: never fake speed you cannot honor.

   *Source: S2, 1.5.*

   - **What:** Productivity soars when system response is under ~400 ms, so neither human nor computer waits on the other *(Doherty & Sørensen, IBM, 1988)*.
   - **Apply:** Sub-400 ms perceived response via optimistic UI, skeleton screens, prefetching; use progress indicators beyond that; animate transitions ≥100 ms so changes are noticed.
   - **Watch:** Never fake speed you can't honor — broken expectations damage trust more than honest load time.

58. **Nielsen's response time limits**

   *Source: S1, line 91.*

   0.1 s feels instant, 1 s keeps flow, 10 s is a classic wait-management heuristic, not a fixed human attention limit. Apply: state beyond 1 s, progress plus async option ("we'll email you") beyond 10 s.

   *Source: S2, 11.1.*

   - **What:** 0.1 s = instant; 1 s = flow limit; 10 s = attention limit *(Nielsen, 1993; still the working standard; Core Web Vitals operationalize it)*.
   - **Apply:** Budget interactions against these bands; beyond 1 s show state; beyond 10 s give progress + async option ("we'll email you when done").

59. **Optimistic UI**

   *Source: S1, line 92.*

   Show success immediately, roll back on failure. Apply: likes, sends, Kanban moves. Watch: not for payments or irreversible actions.

   *Source: S2, 11.2.*

   - **What:** Users judge speed by perception; assuming success and rolling back on failure feels dramatically faster than waiting for confirmation.
   - **Apply:** Optimistic updates for low-risk actions (like, send, add item), instant visual acknowledgment everywhere, prefetch likely-next content.
   - **Watch:** Optimistic UI on payment/irreversible actions is a bug factory — confirm those.

60. **Skeleton screens vs spinners**

   *Source: S1, line 93.*

   Skeletons set expectations and feel shorter; spinners say only "wait". Apply: skeletons for content, spinners for sub-second actions, steps or percent for long jobs; skeleton must match final layout.

   *Source: S2, 11.3.*

   - **What:** Skeletons (shape-preserving placeholders) make waits feel shorter and set expectations; spinners communicate only "wait."
   - **Apply:** Skeletons for content loads, spinners for sub-second actions, percentage/step indicators for long operations; skeletons must match final layout — layout shift after load undoes the benefit.

61. **Progress bar psychology**

   *Source: S1, line 94.*

   Smooth, accelerating bars feel fastest; stalls feel intolerable. Apply: never stick at 90%, show real steps and status text.

   *Source: S2, 11.4.*

   - **What:** Perceived duration depends on bar behavior: smooth accelerating bars feel fastest; stalls are intolerable (perceptual research on progress-bar scaling, e.g., Chris Harrison et al., CMU).
   - **Apply:** Never show a bar stuck at 90%; show real steps ("2 of 5"), combine bar + specific status text; if the tail is genuinely slow, shift perceived progress to remaining steps.

62. **Operational transparency (Buell and Norton, 2011)**

   *Source: S1, line 95.*

   Showing work being done reduces perceived wait and raises value. Apply: live processing logs, courier maps.

   *Source: S2, 11.5.*

   - **What:** Showing the work being done reduces perceived wait and raises value perception *(Buell & Norton's airport baggage-tracker field study, 2011)*.
   - **Apply:** Let users watch the process: live render/scan/processing logs, map of courier, "an agent is reviewing your request" with progress; transparency converts waiting into engagement.

63. **Maister's waiting principles**

   *Source: S1, line 96.*

   Occupied time feels shorter, uncertain waits feel longer than known ones, unexplained longer than explained, unfair longer than fair, solo longer than group, valuable service tolerates longer waits. Apply: give something to do, state expected wait, show queue position, explain causes.

   *Source: S2, 11.6.*

   - **What:** Occupied time feels shorter; people want to start; anxiety makes waits feel longer; uncertain waits > known waits; unexplained > explained; unfair > fair; solo > group; more valuable service → longer tolerated waits *(David Maister, "The Psychology of Waiting Lines," 1985)*.
   - **Apply:** Give users something to do during load (tips, previews), state expected wait explicitly ("usually 30–60 s"), show queue position, explain causes; never leave an unexplained spinner over 10 s.

64. **Duration neglect**

   *Source: S1, line 97.*

   Retrospective judgment cares little about duration, much about peaks and ends. Apply: add a finish ritual to long processes.

   *Source: S2, 11.7.*

   - **What:** In retrospective judgment, duration matters little compared to peaks and ends (part of Peak-End research).
   - **Apply:** Long mediocre onboarding with a strong ending beats short onboarding that trails off; add a finish ritual to long processes (summary of what was accomplished).

65. **Explained uncertainty**

   *Source: S1, line 98.*

   Honest ranges with reasons feel shorter ("3 to 5 min, your file is large"). Proactively update when exceeded.

   *Source: S2, 11.8.*

   - **What:** Unknown-duration waits feel longest; honest ranges with reasons feel shorter.
   - **Apply:** "3–5 min (your file is large)" beats "Processing…" forever-spinner; when delay exceeds the stated range, proactively explain ("still working — 200 pages found").

66. **Gamified waits**

   *Source: S1, line 99.*

   Tips, playful loaders, haptics convert dead time. Keep short waits clean.

   *Source: S2, 11.9.*

   - **What:** Micro-interactions during mandatory waits (pull-to-refresh, playful loaders) convert dead time into engagement.
   - **Apply:** Rotate tips/lore in loaders for repeated long operations; make progress tactile (haptics, springy motion); keep short waits clean — whimsy costs attention.

67. **Flow (Csikszentmihalyi, 1990)**

   *Source: S1, line 100.*

   Deep engagement when challenge slightly exceeds skill with clear goals and immediate feedback. Apply: minimize interruptions, autosave, focus modes, instant feedback.

   *Source: S2, 1.7.*

   - **What:** Deep engagement occurs when challenge slightly exceeds skill, with clear goals and immediate feedback *(Csikszentmihalyi, 1990)*.
   - **Apply:** For creation/coding/analytics tools: minimize interruptions, autosave, hide chrome in focus modes, instant feedback on every action, escape hatches from interruptions ("don't ask now").

68. **Tesler's Law (conservation of complexity)**

   *Source: S1, line 101.*

   Irreducible complexity sits with user or system. Apply: absorb it in the system (smart defaults, automation, templates, advanced mode for those who want it). Watch: over-hiding controls in enterprise software harms usability and mental models.

   *Source: S2, 1.18.*

   - **What:** Every application has an irreducible amount of complexity; someone must absorb it — the system or the user.
   - **Apply:** Move complexity into the system: smart defaults, automation, templates, "one click" versions of expert flows. Push complexity only to those who want it (advanced mode).
   - **Watch:** Absorbed complexity still exists — don't hide so much that users can't build a mental model.

69. **Postel's Law (robustness)**

   *Source: S1, line 102.*

   Be liberal in what you accept, conservative in what you send. Apply: accept phone numbers with any formatting, trim whitespace, validate on blur not keystroke. Watch: validate strictly at persistence boundaries.

   *Source: S2, 1.16.*

   - **What:** "Be liberal in what you accept, conservative in what you send" — forgiving input, strict output.
   - **Apply:** Accept phone numbers with or without formatting, trim whitespace, auto-correct dates; validate on blur, not on keystroke; never punish format variance with errors.
   - **Watch:** 2020s critique: over-tolerant *data* handling (not UI) can normalize bad data — validate strictly at persistence boundaries.

70. **Weber-Fechner (just noticeable difference)**

   *Source: S1, line 103.*

   Perceived change is proportional to baseline. Apply: price increases framed absolute vs relative on purpose; meaningful UI differences must exceed the JND; background changes below it avoid redesign churn.

   *Source: S2, 1.23.*

   - **What:** Perceived change is proportional to the baseline — a 10% price raise on a $5 plan feels different than on a $500 plan.
   - **Apply:** Frame price increases in absolute vs relative terms strategically; make meaningful UI differences exceed the JND (bigger, brighter, distinct), and make background changes *below* the JND to avoid churn-inducing redesigns.

### Attention and perception

71. **Banner blindness**

   *Source: S1, line 107.*

   Anything ad-like is ignored (Benway and Lane, 1998). Apply: critical messages inline in the task flow, system styling. Watch: styling marketing to look like system UI is a disguised ad.

   *Source: S2, 3.1.*

   - **What:** Users habitually ignore anything that looks like an ad or promotional banner *(Benway & Lane, 1998; confirmed by NN/g eye-tracking)*.
   - **Apply:** Critical messages must not look like banners: inline them in the task flow, use system-level styling, or interrupt deliberately (with a dismiss path).
   - **Watch:** The inverse is the dark pattern: styling *marketing* to look like system UI.

72. **Change blindness**

   *Source: S1, line 108.*

   Unanimated or off-attention changes go unnoticed. Apply: animate state changes, highlight what changed ("3 new rows").

   *Source: S2, 3.2.*

   - **What:** Large changes go unnoticed when they happen outside attention or without a visual transition *(Simons & Levin, 1998)*.
   - **Apply:** Animate state changes; highlight what changed in lists/dashboards ("3 new rows"); never swap content in place silently.

73. **Inattentional blindness**

   *Source: S1, line 109.*

   Visible stimuli are missed when attention is elsewhere (invisible gorilla). Apply: do not put critical warnings in task-focused zones; time interstitials to task completion.

   *Source: S2, 3.3.*

   - **What:** People miss a highly visible stimulus when attention is engaged elsewhere *(Simons & Chabris' "invisible gorilla," 1999)*.
   - **Apply:** Don't place critical warnings where users are task-focused; interrupt for destructive confirmation; time interstitials to attention pauses (task completion), not mid-task.

74. **Cocktail party effect**

   *Source: S1, line 110.*

   Personally relevant cues break through. Apply: personalize notifications with the user's own data.

   *Source: S2, 3.4.*

   - **What:** People can tune in to one stream and tune everything else out — but personally relevant cues (their name, their data) break through.
   - **Apply:** Personalize notifications with the user's own data ("Your report is ready"), not generic copy; relevance is the attention filter.

75. **Preattentive processing (pop-out)**

   *Source: S1, line 111.*

   Color, size, orientation, motion are processed in under 200 to 250 ms. Apply: one preattentive attribute per critical element; pop-out fails when many items vary.

   *Source: S2, 3.5.*

   - **What:** Color, size, orientation, and motion are processed in <200–250 ms before conscious attention — a unique dissimilar item "pops out" *(Treisman's feature-integration research)*.
   - **Apply:** Use one preattentive attribute per critical element (a red badge, a moving element); pop-out fails when many items vary simultaneously ("where's Waldo" effect).

76. **Von Restorff (isolation) effect**

   *Source: S1, line 112.*

   The distinct item is remembered. Apply: one accent CTA, one highlighted recommended tier. Watch: highlight everything and nothing stands out; do not highlight the expensive option while hiding the cheap one.

   *Source: S2, 1.19.*

   - **What:** The item that differs most from its surroundings is the one remembered *(von Restorff, 1933)*.
   - **Apply:** One accent color for the primary CTA; highlight the recommended plan; make the one thing that matters visually distinct — and only one.
   - **Watch:** If everything is highlighted, nothing is (dark-pattern misuse: highlighting the expensive option while hiding the cheap one, §14).

77. **F-pattern**

   *Source: S1, line 113.*

   Text-heavy pages are scanned top lines then down the left (NN/g, 2006). Apply: front-load headings and first two words, structured and bolded text.

   *Source: S2, 3.6.*

   - **What:** Text-heavy pages are scanned in an F shape: top lines, then down the left edge *(NN/g eye-tracking, 2006)*.
   - **Apply:** Front-load headings and the first two words of paragraphs/links; put key content and CTAs where the F lands; never assume full reading — bulleted, bolded, structured text survives scanning.

78. **Z-pattern and Gutenberg diagram**

   *Source: S1, line 114.*

   Sparse pages scan in a Z; bottom-left is the dead zone, bottom-right the terminal focus. Apply: logo top-left, CTA top-right or bottom-right.

   *Source: S2, 3.7.*

   - **What:** Sparse visual pages are scanned in a Z; the visual "dead corner" is bottom-left, the terminal focus is bottom-right.
   - **Apply:** Logo/brand top-left, primary CTA top-right or bottom-right (the natural end), nothing critical in the dead zone.

79. **Visual hierarchy and visual weight**

   *Source: S1, line 115.*

   Size, color, contrast, position and density rank importance. Apply: heading, body, meta; one highest-contrast primary CTA.

   *Source: S2, 3.9.*

   - **What:** Size, color, contrast, position, and density assign relative importance; users process in weight order.
   - **Apply:** One clear primary hierarchy per screen: heading > body > meta; control contrast discipline (primary CTA highest contrast on the page).

80. **Mere exposure effect (Zajonc, 1968)**

   *Source: S1, line 116.*

   Repetition increases liking. Apply: consistent brand elements, gradual rebrands. Watch: repeated notification patterns turn into noise.

   *Source: S2, 3.10.*

   - **What:** Repeated exposure increases liking *(Zajonc, 1968)*.
   - **Apply:** Consistent brand elements across touchpoints compound affinity; familiarity of patterns (Jakob's Law) doubles as liking. New logos/colors should be introduced gradually.
   - **Watch:** Fatigue is real — repeating one notification pattern until it's ignored is exposure without delight.

81. **Curiosity gap (Loewenstein, 1994)**

   *Source: S1, line 117.*

   Curiosity comes from a gap between known and wanted knowledge. Apply: specific headlines. Watch: unclosed gaps (clickbait) destroy trust.

   *Source: S2, 3.11.*

   - **What:** Curiosity arises from a gap between what we know and want to know *(Loewenstein, 1994)*.
   - **Apply:** Headlines/subject lines that open a specific gap ("The 3 settings most teams miss"), progress indicators of hidden value ("You have 4 unrevealed insights"), preview with locked remainder.
   - **Watch:** Clickbait that doesn't close the gap destroys trust permanently.

82. **Information scent (Pirolli and Card, 1999)**

   *Source: S1, line 118.*

   Users follow cues to judge target value. Apply: precise labels ("Billing and invoices", not "Account"), result counts, previews.

   *Source: S2, 3.12.*

   - **What:** Users follow cues (link labels, icons, snippets) like animals following scent, judging whether the target is worth the cost *(Pirolli & Card, 1999)*.
   - **Apply:** Labels must promise the destination precisely ("Billing & invoices," not "Account"); show result counts/previews; strengthen scent where users currently drop off.

83. **Saliency and iso-feature suppression**

   *Source: S1, line 119.*

   In V1, repeated same-feature items suppress each other; a unique feature singleton escapes suppression. Apply: saliency is relative (red CTA on blue dashboard works; green on green fails). Top-left quadrant gets the most initial fixations on mobile.

84. **Top-down guidance**

   *Source: S1, line 120.*

   Goal-driven search uses mental templates combined with bottom-up saliency. Apply: align critical elements with salient cues that match what users look for.

85. **Novelty**

   *Source: S1, line 121.*

   Novel stimuli get prioritized processing, with effects dependent on age, timing and task. Apply: separate novelty that supports exploration or learning from endless novelty that just keeps the next item uncertain.

### Memory, learning and experience evaluation

86. **Peak-end rule (Kahneman and Fredrickson, 1993)**

   *Source: S1, line 125.*

   Experiences are judged by the most intense moment and the end, not the average. Apply: engineer peak moments (first success, confetti) and endings (success screens, next-step guidance, warm cancellation); never end a session on an error; end-of-trial and end-of-session screens are high-ROI. Microinteractions (animation, haptics, sound) at peak and end.

   *Source: S2, 1.15.*

   - **What:** People judge an experience by its most intense moment (peak) and its end, not its average *(Kahneman & Fredrickson, 1993)*.
   - **Apply:** Engineer peak moments (celebrate completion, confetti on first success) and endings (success screens, "what next" guidance, warm cancellation flows). Never let a session end on an error.
   - **Watch:** This is why end-of-trial and end-of-session screens are the highest-ROI screens to design.

87. **Serial position effect**

   *Source: S1, line 126.*

   First (primacy) and last (recency) items are remembered best (Murdock, 1962). Apply: critical items first and last in nav, pricing lists, onboarding; the middle is a dead zone.

   *Source: S2, 1.17.*

   - **What:** Items at the beginning (primacy) and end (recency) of a sequence are remembered best *(Murdock, 1962)*.
   - **Apply:** Put the most important items first and last in nav, pricing feature lists, and onboarding steps; the middle is where recall dies.

88. **Zeigarnik effect (1927)**

   *Source: S1, line 127.*

   Unfinished tasks are remembered and create tension. Apply: progress bars, checklists, "60% complete", resume where you left off, badges. Watch: a 2025 meta-analysis found the memory advantage is hard to reproduce; the tendency to resume interrupted tasks is more reliable; a 2026 meta-analysis links unfinished tasks to off-hours rumination.

   *Source: S2, 1.20.*

   - **What:** People remember interrupted, incomplete tasks better than completed ones *(Zeigarnik, 1927)*.
   - **Apply:** Show incomplete states ("Your profile is 60% complete"), persistent task lists, draft-saving, "resume where you left off." Completion drives re-engagement.

89. **Aesthetic-usability effect (Kurosu and Kashimura, 1995)**

   *Source: S1, line 128.*

   Attractive designs are perceived as more usable and forgiven for friction. Apply: invest in polish early; visual quality builds trust. Watch: polish can mask real failures, so measure task success separately.

   *Source: S2, 1.1.*

   - **What:** Users perceive attractive interfaces as *more usable* than they actually are, and tolerate minor flaws in them *(Kurosu & Kashimura, 1995)*.
   - **Apply:** Invest early in visual polish, consistent typography, and brand-aligned aesthetics — beauty buys forgiveness for friction and boosts first impressions in trials and app-store screenshots.
   - **Watch:** Polish can mask real usability failures; keep measuring task success separately from satisfaction.

90. **Forgetting curve (Ebbinghaus, 1885)**

   *Source: S1, line 129.*

   Memory decays fast without reinforcement. Apply: re-teach rare features contextually, spaced re-engagement.

   *Source: S2, 4.2.*

   - **What:** Memory decays exponentially; most is lost within hours/days without reinforcement *(Ebbinghaus, 1885)*.
   - **Apply:** Re-teach rarely used features contextually (empty states, tooltips on first re-encounter); spaced re-engagement emails; never assume learning from one exposure persists.

91. **Spacing effect**

   *Source: S1, line 130.*

   Spaced repetition beats cramming. Apply: scheduled review in learning apps, drip feature education over weeks.

   *Source: S2, 4.3.*

   - **What:** Spaced repetition beats cramming for retention.
   - **Apply:** In learning products (Duolingo model), schedule review sessions; in SaaS, drip feature education over the first weeks instead of one dump.

92. **Testing effect (Roediger and Karpicke, 2006)**

   *Source: S1, line 131.*

   Retrieval beats re-reading. Apply: try-it-yourself steps, micro-challenges.

   *Source: S2, 4.4.*

   - **What:** Actively retrieving information strengthens memory more than re-reading *(Roediger & Karpicke, 2006)*.
   - **Apply:** Quizzes, "try it yourself" steps in onboarding, micro-challenges; make users *do* the feature once — it doubles retention of it.

93. **Verbatim effect**

   *Source: S1, line 132.*

   People remember gist, not wording. Apply: the headline must carry the message; re-show amounts and dates at the moment of action.

   *Source: S2, 4.5.*

   - **What:** People remember the gist/paraphrase, not exact wording.
   - **Apply:** Design copy for gist: the headline must carry the full message alone; important specifics must be re-shown at the moment of action (amounts, dates), never assumed remembered.

94. **Picture superiority effect**

   *Source: S1, line 133.*

   Images are remembered better than words. Apply: diagrams, screenshots, avatars.

   *Source: S2, 4.6.*

   - **What:** Images are remembered far better than words *(Paivio; picture-superiority studies)*.
   - **Apply:** Use diagrams over text for concepts, screenshots over descriptions for features, avatars/photos for people in social features.

95. **Dual coding (Paivio, 1971)**

   *Source: S1, line 134.*

   Verbal plus visual channels strengthen encoding. Apply: pair concepts with icons or diagrams; complement, do not duplicate.

   *Source: S2, 4.7.*

   - **What:** Combining verbal + visual channels increases encoding *(Paivio, 1971)*.
   - **Apply:** Pair every abstract concept with an icon/diagram; onboarding videos with captions hit both channels; don't duplicate identical info in both channels — *complement* it.

96. **Google effect (digital amnesia)**

   *Source: S1, line 135.*

   People forget what is easily retrievable. Apply: searchable history, receipts archive, recently viewed.

   *Source: S2, 4.8.*

   - **What:** People forget information they know they can easily retrieve again *(Sparrow, Liu & Wegner, 2011)*.
   - **Apply:** Don't fight it — support it: make anything findable again (searchable history, receipt archive, "recently viewed"). Friction to re-find what users expect to find is churn.

97. **Misinformation effect (Loftus)**

   *Source: S1, line 136.*

   Later info distorts memory of events. Apply: confirmation dialogs state neutrally what will happen.

   *Source: S2, 4.9.*

   - **What:** Post-event information distorts memory of the event *(Loftus)*.
   - **Apply:** Confirmation dialogs must state what *will* happen, neutrally; don't let leading copy rewrite what users believe they did (matters in finance, deletion, permissions).

98. **Scaffolding (Vygotsky)**

   *Source: S1, line 137.*

   Support that gradually withdraws. Apply: in-context checklists, tooltips that disappear after use.

   *Source: S2, 4.10.*

   - **What:** Skills grow with support that gradually withdraws (Vygotsky's zone of proximal development; consistent with the Paradox of the Active User, 1.21).
   - **Apply:** Teach inside the real task (checklists in-context, pre-filled examples), withdraw help progressively (tooltips disappear after use), difficulty ramps in empty→basic→power states.

99. **Generation effect (Slamecka and Graf, 1978)**

   *Source: S1, line 138.*

   Self-generated answers are remembered better. Apply: have users type their goal, name their own projects.

   *Source: S2, 4.11.*

   - **What:** Self-generated answers are remembered better than read ones *(Slamecka & Graf, 1978)*.
   - **Apply:** Make users type their goal during onboarding (not pick from a list) when retention of intent matters; let users name their projects/workspaces — it increases both memory and ownership (ties to IKEA Effect, 5.15).

100. **Spatial and procedural memory**

   *Source: S1, line 139.*

   see gesture ergonomics in Section 4.

101. **Interrupted-task resumption and off-hours rumination**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   The summary separates resuming interrupted tasks from a universal memory advantage for unfinished tasks. It reports a 2025 meta-analysis qualifying the latter and a 2026 work-psychology meta-analysis associating unfinished tasks with off-hours rumination. These source-reported findings are not independent verification.

### Usability heuristics and mental models

102. **Jakob's Law**

   *Source: S1, line 143.*

   Users spend most time on other products and expect yours to work the same. Apply: follow conventions (cart icon, hamburger menu, top-left logo to home, bottom tab bar on iOS); innovate on differentiating flows only. Violations push users from System 1 to System 2 and raise abandonment.

   *Source: S2, 1.10.*

   - **What:** Users spend most of their time on *other* sites and apps, so they prefer your product to work the same way as the ones they already know *(Jakob Nielsen)*.
   - **Apply:** Follow platform conventions (bottom tab bar on iOS, hamburger/drawer patterns, cart top-right, standard iconography); innovate on the differentiating flows, not the basics.

103. **Mental models**

   *Source: S1, line 144.*

   Compressed representations of how systems work from past experience. Apply: align architecture with them.

104. **Nielsen's 10 heuristics**

   *Source: S1, line 145.*

   1. Visibility of system status (real progress, not a static spinner).
     2. Match between system and real world (familiar language, trash can for delete).
     3. User control and freedom (undo, persistent undo toast after destructive actions).
     4. Consistency and standards (same words, icons and colors mean the same thing).
     5. Error prevention (constraints, defaults, disable invalid submit).
     6. Recognition rather than recall.
     7. Flexibility and efficiency (accelerators like keyboard shortcuts for experts).
     8. Aesthetic and minimalist design.
     9. Help users recognize, diagnose and recover from errors (plain language, precise problem, a solution).
     10. Help and documentation (searchable, contextual).

### Motivation, habit and engagement

105. **Fogg Behavior Model (B = MAP)**

   *Source: S1, line 159.*

   Behavior needs Motivation, Ability and a Prompt together; motivation and ability compensate each other. Apply: diagnose the missing element; failed activation is usually an Ability failure (cut steps, use SSO, prepopulated templates); prompts work best after motivation peaks.
     - **Three motivators**: Sensation (pleasure/pain), Anticipation (hope/fear), Belonging (acceptance/rejection).
     - **Six simplicity factors**: time, money, physical effort, brain cycles, social deviance, non-routine. Fix the cheapest first.
     - **Prompt types**: Spark (low motivation, adds motivation), Facilitator (high motivation, low ability, simplify), Signal (high both, reminder).

   *Source: S2, 7.1.*

   - **What:** Behavior happens when Motivation × Ability × Prompt converge at the same moment *(BJ Fogg, "Persuasive Technology," 2003; "Tiny Habits," 2019)*.
   - **Apply:** For any target action, diagnose the missing ingredient: raise motivation (show value), lower ability cost (fewer steps), fix the prompt (right time, right place). Prompts after motivation peaks (post-win moments) work best.

106. **Hook model (Eyal)**

   *Source: S1, line 163.*

   Trigger, Action, Variable Reward, Investment. External triggers become internal (boredom, loneliness, anxiety). Investment improves the next cycle and loads the next trigger. Watch: Eyal's own test is whether the habit improves the user's life; otherwise it is exploitation.

   *Source: S2, 7.3.*

   - **What:** Nir Eyal's four-phase habit loop for products ("Hooked," 2014): recurring triggers paired with actions leading to variable rewards and stored-value investment.
   - **Apply:** Design the cycle for your product: external trigger (notification) → minimal action (one tap) → variable reward (new content/social/achievement) → investment (data created) that improves the *next* cycle and loads the next trigger.
   - **Watch:** Eyal's own test: does the habit improve the user's life? (His follow-up: "Indistractable," 2019.) Products that fail that test are exploitation (§14).

107. **Habit loop (cue, routine, reward; Duhigg, Wood and Neal)**

   *Source: S1, line 164.*

   Stable context plus consistent reward builds automaticity. Apply: anchor to existing routines. Evidence: habit formation time varies widely and is often months, not a fixed 21 or 66 days.

   *Source: S2, 7.4.*

   - **What:** Habits form from context cues triggering routines rewarded consistently *(Duhigg, 2012; Wood & Neal's context-cue research)*.
   - **Apply:** Anchor your product to existing routines ("with your morning coffee," calendar-adjacent); consistency of cue and reward matters more than reward size; context (same place, same time) is the strongest cue.

108. **COM-B (Capability, Opportunity, Motivation)**

   *Source: S1, line 165.*

   Diagnose whether users lack capability, opportunity or motivation; common in health, fitness and enterprise SaaS.

109. **PSYCH framework**

   *Source: S1, line 166.*

   Personality, Social influence, Yes (rewards), Context, Habits.

110. **Operant conditioning (Skinner)**

   *Source: S1, line 167.*

   Four consequence types in UI.
     - Positive reinforcement: likes, badges, level-ups.
     - Negative reinforcement: clearing a stressful badge, upgrading to remove ads.
     - Positive punishment: ban warning, login lockout.
     - Negative punishment: losing a streak, trial expiry, reduced visibility (shadowbanning).

111. **Schedules of reinforcement**

   *Source: S1, line 172.*

   Continuous reinforcement teaches fast but extinguishes fast. Variable ratio (pull-to-refresh, infinite feed, loot boxes) resists extinction; variable interval (random notifications) drives steady checking. Watch: "variable rewards cause addiction" is an overstatement (Section 17).

112. **Variable rewards (Eyal)**

   *Source: S1, line 173.*

   Tribe (social), hunt (search or collection), self (mastery or novelty). Habituation and reinforcement effects depend on context; no fixed-days rule is established here.

   *Source: S2, 7.5.*

   - **What:** Unpredictable rewards sustain engagement far more than fixed ones *(Skinner's operant conditioning)*; Eyal's three types: rewards of the tribe (social), the hunt (search/collection), the self (mastery/novelty).
   - **Apply:** Vary the payload: new follower counts, fresh insights each Monday, rotating templates; fixed rewards habituate within days.
   - **Watch:** This is the slot-machine mechanic — pair with real user value and controls (notification digests, session reminders), or it becomes a §14 item.

113. **Dopamine reward prediction error**

   *Source: S1, line 174.*

   Dopamine signals unexpected better or worse outcomes and drives seeking, not just pleasure. Unexpected rewards spike it; missing predicted rewards drop it below baseline. Watch: "dopamine equals pleasure" is a popular misreading.

114. **Investment and stored value**

   *Source: S1, line 175.*

   Each session should deepen stored data, history, integrations or reputation. Stored value, not lock-in, is durable retention.

   *Source: S2, 7.6.*

   - **What:** Each cycle should load the next: user-added data, content, connections, preferences.
   - **Apply:** Map your product's stored value (docs, integrations, history, reputation, customization) and make every session deepen at least one; stored value — not lock-in contracts — is durable retention.

115. **Self-Determination Theory (Deci and Ryan)**

   *Source: S1, line 176.*

   Autonomy (real choice, customization), Competence (progressive challenge, visible growth), Relatedness (teams, sharing, community). Evidence: small but significant behavioral effects in a meta-analysis of 65 SDT interventions.

   *Source: S2, 7.7.*

   - **What:** Intrinsic motivation grows when three needs are met *(Deci & Ryan, 1985/2000)*.
   - **Apply:** Autonomy: real choices, customization, opt-ins. Competence: progressive difficulty, visible skill growth, good defaults that make users feel smart. Relatedness: teams, sharing, community, co-editing. Products serving all three retain without reward inflation.

116. **Overjustification effect (Lepper, Greene and Nisbett, 1973)**

   *Source: S1, line 177.*

   Extrinsic rewards can erode intrinsic motivation. Apply: use points where a spark is needed and taper them; praise mastery over activity.

   *Source: S2, 7.8.*

   - **What:** Extrinsic rewards can *destroy* intrinsic motivation *(Lepper, Greene & Nisbett, 1973 — children's drawing study)*.
   - **Apply:** Don't pay users for what they already enjoy; use badges/points for behaviors that need a spark, and taper them as intrinsic value takes over; praise progress and mastery, not mere activity.

117. **Progress principle (Amabile and Kramer, 2011)**

   *Source: S1, line 178.*

   Small visible progress in meaningful work is the strongest daily motivator. Apply: weekly recaps, "3 tasks shipped today", autosave and undo to prevent setbacks.

   *Source: S2, 7.9.*

   - **What:** Small, visible progress in meaningful work is the strongest daily motivator *(Amabile & Kramer, "The Progress Principle," 2011)*.
   - **Apply:** Surface small wins: "3 tasks shipped today," weekly recap emails, momentum stats; protect users from setbacks (autosave, undo) — setbacks deflate motivation disproportionately.

118. **Goal-gradient effect (Hull, 1932; Kivetz et al., 2006)**

   *Source: S1, line 179.*

   Effort accelerates near a goal. Apply: "7 of 10 done", quick wins first, "X% to free shipping".

   *Source: S2, 1.8.*

   - **What:** Effort accelerates as people approach a goal *(Hull, 1932; loyalty-card field study: Kivetz, Urminsky & Zheng, 2006)*.
   - **Apply:** Show progress ("7 of 10 done"), place progress bars so the first segment fills fastest, sort onboarding so quick wins come first, show "X% to free shipping."

119. **Endowed progress effect (Nunes and Dreze, 2006)**

   *Source: S1, line 180.*

   Credited head-start raises completion (pre-stamped car-wash cards 34% vs 19%). Apply: onboarding checklist starting with items already done, prefilled profiles from imports. Watch: progress must be truthful; fabricated progress is deception.

   *Source: S2, 6.9.*

   - **What:** Artificial advancement toward a goal increases completion *(Nunes & Drèze, 2006: car-wash cards pre-stamped completed 34% vs 19%)*.
   - **Apply:** Start progress bars at 20% ("Step 1 of 5" pre-done), prefill profiles with imported data, "We've imported your contacts — finish in 2 clicks." Honest version: credit progress that reflects real work done *for* the user.
   - **Watch:** Fabricated progress on things the user never did crosses into deception.

120. **Streaks and loss-framed momentum (Duolingo)**

   *Source: S1, line 181.*

   The streak becomes an asset to protect. Apply: streak freezes and repair, milestone celebration. Watch: guilt-based streak shaming; streak loss can be coercive.

   *Source: S2, 7.10.*

   - **What:** Streak mechanics (Duolingo) weaponize loss aversion for consistency: the streak itself becomes the asset to protect.
   - **Apply:** Offer streak freezes/repairs for genuine absence; celebrate milestone streaks; pair streak risk with the actual value ("keep your streak — 2 minutes today").
   - **Watch:** Guilt-based streak shaming is a documented dark pattern adjacent to confirmshaming.

121. **Gamification (points, badges, leaderboards)**

   *Source: S1, line 182.*

   Effects are small and depend on the specific element. Apply: cooperative or past-self comparisons, badges tied to competence, meaningful points. Watch: bottom-of-leaderboard demotivation; badge inflation; not a guaranteed effect.

   *Source: S2, 7.11.*

   - **What:** PBL systems can bootstrap engagement — and backfire: leaderboards demotivate the majority at the bottom; badges inflate worthless behavior.
   - **Apply:** Use cooperative/relative-progress leaderboards (teams, past-self comparison); meaningful badges tied to competence (SDT 7.7); give points *meaning* (redeemable, status-bearing) or skip points entirely.

122. **Customization and ownership**

   *Source: S1, line 183.*

   Early customization builds investment and autonomy. Apply: ship good defaults so customization is optional.

   *Source: S2, 7.12.*

   - **What:** Configuring one's environment raises both ownership (endowment 5.16) and autonomy (7.7).
   - **Apply:** Early customization moments (theme, workspace name, avatar) create investment cheaply; ship sane defaults so customization is optional delight, not a required chore.

123. **Fresh start effect (Dai, Milkman and Riis, 2014)**

   *Source: S1, line 184.*

   Temporal landmarks boost aspirational behavior. Apply: Monday, new year, birthday campaigns; year-in-review.

   *Source: S2, 6.8.*

   - **What:** Temporal landmarks (Monday, new year, birthday) boost aspirational behavior and conversion *(Dai, Milkman & Riis, 2014)*.
   - **Apply:** Time re-engagement campaigns to landmarks ("Start your fresh start this Monday"); "New year, new workspace" onboarding; year-in-review summaries as renewal moments.

124. **Commitment devices and deadlines (Ariely and Wertenbroch, 2002)**

   *Source: S1, line 185.*

   Self-chosen precommitment raises completion. Apply: user-set deadlines, stakes, email-my-future-self.

   *Source: S2, 6.7.*

   - **What:** Voluntary pre-commitment (stakes, deadlines, public pledges) sharply raises goal completion *(Ariely & Wertenbroch, 2002)*.
   - **Apply:** Let users set their own deadline ("I'll publish by Friday"), email-my-future-self, deposit/stake mechanics; commitment works best when *chosen*, not imposed.

125. **Temptation bundling (Milkman, Minson and Volpp, 2014)**

   *Source: S1, line 186.*

   Pair a should with a want.

126. **Effort heuristic (Kruger et al., 2004)**

   *Source: S1, line 187.*

   More apparent effort means more perceived value. Apply: visible processing steps, "analyzing your 340 data points".

   *Source: S2, 6.10.*

   - **What:** More apparent effort → higher perceived value *(Kruger et al., 2004)*.
   - **Apply:** Show the craft: "hand-crafted report for you," render/processing steps visible, personalized computations ("analyzing your 340 data points…") — perceived effort raises willingness to pay and patience.

127. **Anticipation**

   *Source: S1, line 188.*

   Waiting for a reward activates reward circuitry. Apply: waitlist positions, scheduled weekly reports, visible feature progress.

   *Source: S2, 8.7.*
