   - **What:** Anticipating a reward activates reward circuitry nearly as much as receiving it; anticipation windows are emotionally valuable *(Kahneman's "pleasures of anticipation")*.
   - **Apply:** Announce upcoming features with visible progress ("shipping in 2 weeks"); waitlists with position numbers; scheduled deliveries (weekly report Mondays) create positive anticipation loops.

128. **Intrinsic vs extrinsic framing of engagement**

   *Source: S1, line 189.*

   Distinguish engagement, habitual use, problematic use and clinical addiction. The summary specifically discusses WHO ICD-11 gaming disorder; "smartphone addiction" and "social-media addiction" are less standardized.

129. **Fogg's Six Ability Factors**

   *Source: S2, 7.2.*

   - **What:** Simplicity = time, money, physical effort, brain cycles, social deviance, and non-routineness all being low.
   - **Apply:** Checklist every flow against the six: How many minutes? What cost? Typing/holding effort? How much thinking? Does it feel socially odd? Is it familiar? Remove the *cheapest* to fix first.

130. **Reinforcement learning**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   Learning from consequences and updating expected outcomes is a mechanism underlying several supplied habit accounts. Reward prediction error, schedules of reinforcement, and operant consequences are related concepts, not interchangeable labels.

131. **Self-monitoring and self-chosen goals**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   The habit summary names self-monitoring, goal setting, cues, feedback, and self-chosen goals as commonly used mechanisms in digital behaviour-change interventions. Stable contexts and repeated action matter; frequency alone does not diagnose addiction.

### Pricing and economic psychology

132. **Mental accounting (Thaler)**

   *Source: S1, line 193.*

   People keep money in buckets. Apply: price in the buyer's budget unit ("$12/user/month, billed annually").

   *Source: S2, 6.3.*

   - **What:** People categorize money into mental buckets ("monthly budget," "business spend") and treat them differently *(Thaler, 1985/1999)*.
   - **Apply:** Price in the customer's accounting frame: per-user/per-month for team tools (fits "operating budget"), one-time for tools; show costs in the units users budget with ("$12/user/month, billed annually").

133. **Zero-price effect (Shampanier, Mazar and Ariely, 2007)**

   *Source: S1, line 194.*

   Free is a different regime from cheap. Apply: real free tiers for network products, upgrade triggers from usage not nagging.

   *Source: S2, 6.4.*

   - **What:** "Free" is not just a low price — it's a different psychological regime; demand jumps disproportionately *(Shampanier, Mazar & Ariely, 2007)*.
   - **Apply:** A genuinely free tier beats a cheap trial for driving adoption of network products; use free for one side of a marketplace; beware: zero attracts non-buyers too — design upgrade triggers from usage, not nagging.

134. **Partitioned and drip pricing (Morwitz et al., 1998)**

   *Source: S1, line 195.*

   Late mandatory fees raise drop-off and complaints. Apply: show all-in price early. Regulatory: active enforcement (FTC junk fees, EU price rules).

135. **Bundling**

   *Source: S1, line 196.*

   Hides unit prices and raises perceived value.

136. **Currency confusion**

   *Source: S1, line 197.*

   Virtual currencies (gems, coins) hide real spend and lower purchase friction. Watch: dark pattern in in-app economies.

137. **Pricing page psychology**

   *Source: S1, line 198.*

   Anchor, decoy and compromise effects combined with Von Restorff highlight on the middle tier drive ARPU.

138. **Nudge theory (Thaler and Sunstein)**

   *Source: S1, line 199.*

   Every default, order and frame is a nudge. Audit and set them in the user's interest. Nudges guide toward good outcomes; sludge adds friction against the user's interest.

   *Source: S2, 6.1.*

   - **What:** Small changes in how choices are presented reliably alter behavior without forbidding options *(Thaler & Sunstein, "Nudge," 2008)*.
   - **Apply:** Every default, order, and frame is a nudge whether you chose it or not — audit them deliberately and set them in the user's interest (better defaults, safer preselections).

139. **Choice architecture and friction design**

   *Source: S1, line 200.*

   Add friction where errors are expensive (delete, transfers, with undo), remove it where value is clear (signup, upgrade). Friction asymmetry is the core skill.

   *Source: S2, 6.2.*

   - **What:** The same options, ordered/packaged differently, produce different choices; friction is a design material.
   - **Apply:** Map friction deliberately: *add* friction where errors are expensive (delete, transfer money — with undo), *remove* friction where value is clear (signup, upgrade). Friction asymmetry is the core skill of flow design.

140. **Partitioned Pricing & Drip Pricing**

   *Source: S2, 6.5.*

   - **What:** Splitting price into components changes perception — and adding mandatory fees late in checkout ("drip pricing") inflates drop-off and complaint rates *(Morwitz, Greenleaf & Johnson, 1998)*.
   - **Apply:** Show the full, all-in price early and everywhere; itemize *with* the total visible. Regulatory note: drip pricing is under active enforcement (FTC junk-fees rulemaking; EU price-indication rules).

141. **Bundling & Temptation Bundling**

   *Source: S2, 6.6.*

   - **What:** Bundles hide individual prices and raise perceived value; pairing a "should" activity with a "want" activity increases follow-through *(Milkman, Minson & Volpp, 2014 — gym audiobook study)*.
   - **Apply:** Bundle adjacent tools ("Editor + Storage"); pair desired-but-avoided behaviors with pleasures: listen to your show only at the gym, weekly report only with your coffee ritual; "cold turkey" commitments (hard blocking) work for high-willpower-needs cases.

### Persuasion and influence (Cialdini plus)

142. **Reciprocity**

   *Source: S1, line 204.*

   Give valuable things first (calculators, templates, audits, surprise upgrades).

   *Source: S2, 9.1.*

   - **What:** We repay gifts, favors, and concessions *(Regan, 1971)*. Personalized and unexpected gifts are strongest.
   - **Apply:** Free valuable tools/content before asking (calculators, templates, audits), surprise upgrades ("we added you Pro for a month"), personalized reports as gifts. Concession form: when user declines big ask, follow with the small one.

143. **Commitment and consistency**

   *Source: S1, line 205.*

   Small, active, self-written commitments first; reference them at decision points.

   *Source: S2, 9.2.*

   - **What:** Once committed (small public/effortful step), people align later behavior with it.
   - **Apply:** Ask for small commitments first (goal selection in onboarding → plan recommendation), make commitments active and self-written ("I want to grow my newsletter"), reference them at decision points ("You told us your goal is X — Pro is the fit").

144. **Social proof**

   *Source: S1, line 206.*

   Copy similar others, especially under uncertainty or high load. Apply: specific logos, counts, testimonials with names and roles ("3,200 dental clinics" beats "thousands"). Evidence: a meta-analysis of 250 effect sizes found social norms influence consumer behavior with moderators. Watch: fabricated counts are deception.

   *Source: S2, 9.3.*

   - **What:** We copy what similar others do, especially under uncertainty *(Asch conformity; Cialdini)*.
   - **Apply:** Testimonials with faces/names/roles, usage counters, "teams like yours use X," live activity ("42 designers signed up this week"), reviews from the user's industry. Specificity beats volume ("used by 3,200 dental clinics" > "thousands").

145. **Authority**

   *Source: S1, line 207.*

   Credentials, SOC 2, expert endorsements, cited ROI. Must be verifiable. A 2026 meta-analysis found only a small average effect from credible sources, so treat as conditional.

   *Source: S2, 9.4.*

   - **What:** Credible expertise and badges increase compliance (Cialdini; the honest form of authority signaling, not the Milgram abuse).
   - **Apply:** Certifications, "SOC 2," expert endorsements with credentials, data citations, team expertise pages. Authority must be verifiable — fake badges are fraud.

146. **Liking**

   *Source: S1, line 208.*

   Similarity, compliments, familiarity, politeness in microcopy.

   *Source: S2, 9.5.*

   - **What:** We say yes to people/products we like: similarity, compliments, familiarity, shared goals.
   - **Apply:** Match onboarding copy to the user's stated identity; human faces and founder voice; "people like you" personalization; politeness and micro-appreciation in microcopy ("Thanks for waiting").

147. **Scarcity**

   *Source: S1, line 209.*

   Scarce means valuable. A meta-analysis of 131 studies found demand, supply and time scarcity differ by context. Apply: only real seat limits, real deadlines, and explain why. Watch: resetting countdowns and fake "only 2 left" are dark patterns.

   *Source: S2, 9.6.*

   - **What:** Scarce items seem more valuable *(Worchel, Lee & Adewole's cookie-jar studies, 1975)*.
   - **Apply:** Honest scarcity only: real seat limits, genuine beta caps, true deadlines (price changes you actually make). Show *why* it's scarce.
   - **Watch:** Fake countdown timers that reset are the canonical dark pattern — enforcement target (FTC actions, §14).

148. **Unity (Cialdini's 7th principle)**

   *Source: S1, line 210.*

   Shared identity is stronger than liking ("built for indie devs, by indie devs").

   *Source: S2, 9.7.*

   - **What:** Shared identity ("one of us": family, region, profession, fandom) is more powerful than liking *(Cialdini, "Pre-Suasion," 2016; Influence 7th ed., 2021)*.
   - **Apply:** Speak in-group: "built for indie devs, by indie devs"; community brands, user-created conventions, insider language, regional/localized identity cues. Unity is the strongest lever in community-led growth.

149. **Foot-in-the-door (Freedman and Fraser, 1966)**

   *Source: S1, line 211.*

   Small compliance leads to larger. Each step needs standalone value.

   *Source: S2, 9.8.*

   - **What:** Small initial compliance increases later larger compliance *(Freedman & Fraser, 1966)*.
   - **Apply:** Free-tool → email → trial → paid ladders; micro-conversions in onboarding (name workspace → invite teammate → upgrade invite). Each step must deliver standalone value.

150. **Door-in-the-face (Cialdini, 1975)**

   *Source: S1, line 212.*

   A refused large ask raises compliance with a smaller one. Use sparingly.

   *Source: S2, 9.9.*

   - **What:** A large request, refused, raises compliance with a smaller follow-up (Cialdini et al., 1975).
   - **Apply:** Downsells after upgrade declines: "Enterprise?" → "Then Pro annual?" in-app offers; use sparingly — visible manipulation breeds distrust.

151. **Fear appeals (EPPM, Witte, 1992)**

   *Source: S1, line 213.*

   Fear works only with high perceived efficacy; give a clear doable action.

   *Source: S2, 9.10.*

   - **What:** Fear motivates only when paired with high perceived efficacy — a clear, doable action (Extended Parallel Process Model, Witte, 1992).
   - **Apply:** Security/backup/compliance messaging: threat + "here's exactly what to do now" + proof it's easy. Fear without efficacy produces avoidance, not action.

152. **Authority bias and social-proof under uncertainty**

   *Source: S1, line 214.*

   see above; users lean on others when expertise is lacking.

### Social and group psychology

153. **Conformity (Asch, 1951)**

   *Source: S1, line 218.*

   People align with the group. Apply: visible norms, pinned rules. Watch: conformity pressure suppressing legitimate dissent.

   *Source: S2, 12.1.*

   - **What:** People align with group answers even against their own eyes *(Asch, 1951)* — normative influence is powerful in visible communities.
   - **Apply:** Community norms self-police when made visible (pinned rules, upvoted conduct); design group-setting defaults toward the behavior you want.
   - **Watch:** Conformity pressure used to suppress legitimate dissent in communities is a moderation failure.

154. **Social comparison theory (Festinger, 1954)**

   *Source: S1, line 219.*

   Upward comparison can motivate or discourage; downward comparison gives temporary esteem. Apply: compare to attainable targets (past self, cohort, next rank). Watch: unwinnable benchmarking, curated feeds, mental-health costs.

   *Source: S2, 12.2.*

   - **What:** We evaluate ourselves against others *(Festinger, 1954)* — upward comparison motivates or discourages depending on perceived attainability.
   - **Apply:** Compare users to *attainable* targets: past self, cohort average, "next rank"; never expose raw leaderboards to the bottom decile without a relevant metric ("fastest progress this week").
   - **Watch:** Upward comparison at scale has documented mental-health costs — design comparison with empathy.

155. **FOMO (Przybylski et al., 2013)**

   *Source: S1, line 220.*

   Anxiety about missing out drives checking. A 2024 meta-analysis (252,337 participants) found moderate associations with problematic social network use, mostly correlational. Watch: manufactured FOMO ("X just bought!").

   *Source: S2, 12.3.*

   - **What:** Anxiety about missing rewarding experiences drives checking behavior *(Przybylski et al., 2013)*.
   - **Apply:** Honest use: real-time status ("3 seats left in this cohort"), visible activity of connections, limited-time real events.
   - **Watch:** Manufactured FOMO (endless fake "X just bought!") is one of the most-reported dark-pattern categories.

156. **Belonging and relatedness**

   *Source: S1, line 221.*

   Roles, rituals, mutual-aid visibility; onboard into community before asking for contributions.

   *Source: S2, 12.4.*

   - **What:** Belonging is a core motivator (SDT 7.7; Baumeister & Leary's belongingness hypothesis, 1995).
   - **Apply:** Give members identity (roles, badges, spaces), rituals (weekly threads), and visible mutual aid (answered-questions counts); onboard into community *before* asking contributions.

157. **Collaboration and shared value**

   *Source: S1, line 222.*

   Invite inside the value moment, show presence, graceful view-only landing for non-users.

   *Source: S2, 12.5.*

   - **What:** Features with two-person value loops (invite, co-edit, share) create network retention.
   - **Apply:** Design the invite moment inside the value moment ("Share this board — collaborators see changes live"), show collaborator presence, give non-users a graceful view-only landing.

158. **Social feedback as reward**

   *Source: S1, line 223.*

   Likes activate reward-related regions (adolescent fMRI studies). Relevant to counters, leaderboards, adolescent design.

159. **Social desirability bias (research)**

   *Source: S1, line 224.*

   Participants report what sounds good. Ask about past behavior, triangulate with analytics.

   *Source: S2, 12.6.*

   - **What:** Research participants report what sounds good, not what they do.
   - **Apply:** Ask about past behavior, not future intent ("When did you last…?" not "Would you…?"); triangulate self-report with analytics; treat stated preferences as hypotheses.

160. **Personalization / personality fit**

   *Source: S1, line 225.*

   Introverts may prefer solo curated experiences and desaturated, harmonious visuals; extroverts community and stimulation (PSYCH, color research).

### Emotion, aesthetics and delight

161. **Norman's three levels of emotional design**

   *Source: S1, line 229.*

   Visceral (look), behavioral (use), reflective (meaning and identity). Products with only visceral appeal churn after week one.

   *Source: S2, 8.1.*

   - **What:** Visceral (immediate appearance), behavioral (use and feel), reflective (meaning, memory, identity) *(Don Norman, "Emotional Design," 2004)*.
   - **Apply:** Visceral: hero screens, app icons, motion polish. Behavioral: frictionless flows. Reflective: annual summaries ("Your year in review"), shareable artifacts, brand rituals. Products that hit only visceral churn after week one.

162. **Microinteractions and delight**

   *Source: S1, line 230.*

   Purposeful animation, haptics, one signature moment per core flow. Never add latency to critical paths.

163. **Voice and humor**

   *Source: S1, line 231.*

   Playful in empty states and success; strictly clear in errors, billing, security.

   *Source: S2, 8.3.*

   - **What:** A consistent, human voice (Mailchimp's tone) raises memorability and tolerance.
   - **Apply:** Define voice per context: playful in empty states and success; strictly clear in errors, billing, security. Humor in error messages must still explain the fix.

164. **Emotional contagion**

   *Source: S1, line 232.*

   Tone transfers through copy, cues and others' behavior.

   *Source: S2, 8.5.*

   - **What:** Emotional states transfer through design cues, copy, and other users' visible behavior.
   - **Apply:** Positive framing in shared spaces (celebrations, supportive defaults in communities); calm, confident copy in onboarding; audit support-ticket flows — contagious frustration is real.

165. **Peak-moment design (Heath and Heath, 2017)**

   *Source: S1, line 233.*

   Build moments of elevation, insight, pride, connection.

   *Source: S2, 8.6.*

   - **What:** Memorable experiences are engineered around elevation, insight, pride, and connection *(Chip & Dan Heath, "The Power of Moments," 2017)* — consistent with the Peak-End Rule (1.15).
   - **Apply:** Deliberately build one peak per milestone: first-value moment celebration, milestone emails with personalized stats, upgrade moments ("you just did what used to take an hour").

166. **Cognitive dissonance and post-purchase reassurance (Festinger, 1957)**

   *Source: S1, line 234.*

   Re-sell the decision after purchase to prevent refund and churn.

   *Source: S2, 8.8.*

   - **What:** After significant choices, people seek reassurance to resolve dissonance *(Festinger, 1957)*; unresolved doubt becomes refund/churn.
   - **Apply:** Post-purchase confirmation must re-sell the decision ("Great choice — here's what happens next"), onboarding email within minutes, visible "what's included" recap; kill buyer's remorse before it kills the subscription.

167. **Emotionally intelligent interfaces (2026)**

   *Source: S1, line 235.*

   Systems read scroll velocity, tap pressure, hesitation to adapt tone, load and layout. Watch: the same sensing can exploit high-impulsivity states for monetization.

168. **Micro-interactions & Delight**

   *Source: S2, 8.2.*

   - **What:** Small functional moments (pull-to-refresh, like animations) carry personality and feedback.
   - **Apply:** Animate state changes with purpose (success check, haptics on mobile); one delightful signature moment per core flow; delight must never add latency to critical paths (Doherty 1.5).

169. **Color Psychology (use with caution)**

   *Source: S2, 8.4.*

   - **What:** Colors carry learned associations (blue=trust/finance, red=alarm/urgency, green=success/go) — the evidence is contextual and culture-dependent, not universal.
   - **Apply:** Use conventions users already know (Jakob's Law 1.10): red for destructive, green for confirm; semantic color consistency beats symbolic color cleverness; always pair color with text/icon (accessibility, color-blindness ~8% of men).

### Trust, credibility and error handling

170. **Stanford Web Credibility Guidelines**

   *Source: S1, line 239.*

   Real-world feel, ease of use, expertise, polish. Typos, broken links and hype hurt fastest. Audit: visible contact info, real photos, dates, no overclaims.

   *Source: S2, 10.1.*

   - **What:** Stanford Persuasive Tech Lab's research (Fogg et al.): real-world feel, ease of use, expertise, trust seals, professional polish drive credibility; typos, broken links, and hype destroy it fastest.
   - **Apply:** Credibility audit: contact info visible, no dead ends, real photos over stock, accurate copy, updated copyright/dates, no overclaiming.

171. **Trust seals and checkout validation**

   *Source: S1, line 240.*

   One relevant seal near payment, reviews near the CTA. Too many or irrelevant badges can depress trust.

   *Source: S2, 10.2.*

   - **What:** Security badges and reviews measurably lift checkout completion — but effects vary; irrelevant or excessive badges can depress trust.
   - **Apply:** One relevant seal near payment (payment provider, "SSL-secured"), reviews near the CTA, money-back guarantee stated exactly; don't carpet-checkout with ten badges.

172. **Transparency as differentiator**

   *Source: S1, line 241.*

   Show "why" behind recommendations, open pricing, public changelogs, admit outages with cause and fix.

   *Source: S2, 10.3.*

   - **What:** Visible reasoning, open pricing, public changelogs, and admitting limits raise long-term trust even when short-term conversion drops.
   - **Apply:** Show "why" behind recommendations ("because you invoice monthly"), publish pricing without "Contact sales" gates where possible, admit outages with cause and fix.

173. **Privacy control and the privacy paradox**

   *Source: S1, line 242.*

   Users say they care, act otherwise, then overcorrect after breaches. Apply: contextual permission asks, plain-language explanations, granular toggles, easy export and deletion.

   *Source: S2, 10.4.*

   - **What:** Users say they care about privacy but behave otherwise — until trust is broken, then they overcorrect.
   - **Apply:** Progressive permission (ask at context: mic when recording), plain-language data explanations, granular toggles, easy export/deletion. Control *perception* and reality must match; dark-pattern data grabs are the fastest trust destroyer.

174. **Error prevention (poka-yoke)**

   *Source: S1, line 243.*

   Constrain inputs, confirm destructive actions by consequence ("Deletes 3 projects"), undo everywhere, disable invalid options.

   *Source: S2, 10.5.*

   - **What:** Preventing errors beats fixing them *(Norman's design principles; industrial poka-yoke)*.
   - **Apply:** Constrain inputs (date pickers over text), confirm destructive actions by consequence ("Deletes 3 projects"), undo everywhere, disable invalid options instead of erroring later.

175. **Blame-free error messages**

   *Source: S1, line 244.*

   What happened, why, what to do now, an escape. Never blame, jargon or dead ends.

   *Source: S2, 10.6.*

   - **What:** Errors written as user fault ("Invalid input!") raise frustration; system-accountability framing ("We couldn't reach the server") preserves trust.
   - **Apply:** Formula: what happened (plain language) + why (briefly) + what to do now (one action) + escape ("Try again / Contact support"). Never blame, never jargon, never dead-end.

176. **Service recovery paradox**

   *Source: S1, line 245.*

   A well-handled failure can raise loyalty (limits: works once, not for catastrophic failures).

   *Source: S2, 10.7.*

   - **What:** A well-handled failure can leave users *more* loyal than no failure at all — with limits (works once; catastrophic failures don't recover).
   - **Apply:** Over-invest in failure journeys: instant acknowledgment, visible fix progress, compensation proportionate to harm ("sorry" credits); the recovery experience is a designed peak (1.15).

177. **Consistency builds trust**

   *Source: S1, line 246.*

   Same terms everywhere, changelogs and deprecation notices when patterns change.

   *Source: S2, 10.8.*

   - **What:** Reliability across time and touchpoints is the cognitive basis of trust: predictable = safe.
   - **Apply:** Identical terms for identical things across product/emails/ads; don't redesign interaction patterns casually; changelog + deprecation notices when patterns must change.

178. **Third-party validation**

   *Source: S1, line 247.*

   Dated audits ("SOC 2 Type II, 2025"), real rating distributions, review widgets.

   *Source: S2, 10.9.*

   - **What:** Independent verification (certifications, press, app-store ratings, G2/Capterra) is trusted more than self-claims.
   - **Apply:** Show verification with date ("SOC 2 Type II, audited 2025"), real rating distributions (not just 4.9 stars), embed review widgets on pricing pages.

179. **Perceived security**

   *Source: S1, line 248.*

   Mask sensitive data, show session and device lists, last login, explicit state. Signals must match real protections.

   *Source: S2, 10.10.*

   - **What:** Users judge security by signals (padlocks, masks, fingerprints) that may not correlate with actual security.
   - **Apply:** Mask sensitive data by default, show session/device lists, display last-login, use explicit state ("Locked — 2FA on"). Signals must reflect real protections; decorative security is a deception (§14).

180. **Progressive permission**

   *Source: S1, line 249.*

   Ask at the moment of need with a rationale; make denial safe and revocable.

   *Source: S2, 10.11.*

   - **What:** Asking for everything at signup (notifications, contacts, location) maximizes denial; contextual asks convert *(NN/g guidance)*.
   - **Apply:** Ask at the moment of need with pre-ask rationale ("We'll need notifications to alert you when the build finishes — enable?"); make denial safe and revocable.

### AI-era human-AI interaction

181. **Shift from command to intent**

   *Source: S1, line 253.*

   Users specify outcomes, AI chooses the path. Lowers effort but reduces felt control.

182. **Automation bias (Skitka and Mosier, 1996)**

   *Source: S1, line 254.*

   Over-trusting automated output. Apply: sources, confidence, confirmation for consequential actions, spot-check prompts. Watch: the core liability of 2024 to 2026 AI features is confident, fluent, wrong output.

   *Source: S2, 13.3.*

   - **What:** Over-trusting automated output: people accept machine answers with less scrutiny than human answers *(Skitka & Mosier, 1996)* — the opposite failure mode of aversion.
   - **Apply:** Build verification into the flow: show sources/confidence, require confirmation for consequential outputs (payments, sends, deletions), occasional spot-check prompts ("does this summary look right?").
   - **Watch:** This is the core liability of AI features in 2024–2026: confident, fluent, *wrong* output accepted silently.

183. **Automation complacency**

   *Source: S1, line 255.*

   Reliable systems erode vigilance. Apply: vary interaction, meaningful human checkpoints.

   *Source: S2, 13.4.*

   - **What:** Prolonged high reliability breeds attention drift — monitoring collapses exactly when it's needed (aviation research, transferred to AI copilots).
   - **Apply:** Vary the interaction (occasional checks, active feedback requests), avoid full auto-pilot modes for consequential flows; design "human checkpoints" that are meaningful, not ritual.

184. **Algorithm aversion (Dietvorst et al., 2015)**

   *Source: S1, line 256.*

   Confidence in algorithms drops faster than in humans after the same error. Apply: honest accuracy framing, one-tap recovery.

   *Source: S2, 13.1.*

   - **What:** People lose confidence in algorithmic advice much faster than in human advice after seeing the same error *(Dietvorst, Simmons & Massey, 2015)*.
   - **Apply:** Design AI failure communication explicitly: frame accuracy honestly upfront ("right ~80% of the time — always review"), make error recovery one tap, never let the first user exposure be a high-stakes failure.

185. **Algorithm appreciation (Logg et al., 2019)**

   *Source: S1, line 257.*

   Machine-labeled advice can be rated higher, depending on domain. Calibrate disclosure per feature.

   *Source: S2, 13.2.*

   - **What:** The countervailing finding: when advice is presented as machine-generated, people rate it *higher* than identical human advice *(Logg, Minson & Moore, 2019)*.
   - **Apply:** In domains where your AI is genuinely strong (matching, forecasting, ranking), *labeled* AI output increases uptake; in judgment-heavy domains (health, hiring, law), the same label triggers aversion — calibrate disclosure per feature.

186. **Trust calibration (Lee and See, 2004)**

   *Source: S1, line 258.*

   Aim for appropriate reliance, not maximum trust. Communicate capability boundaries, show uncertainty, make rejecting and editing easy.

   *Source: S2, 13.5.*

   - **What:** The design goal is neither max trust nor min trust, but *appropriate* reliance *(Lee & See, 2004)* — trust proportional to actual system capability.
   - **Apply:** Communicate capability boundaries ("great at summarizing, unreliable with numbers"), show uncertainty visually (confidence bars, source links), make it easy to reject and edit output — calibrated users become power users; miscalibrated users churn or get harmed.

187. **ELIZA effect (Weizenbaum, 1966)**

   *Source: S1, line 259.*

   People attribute understanding to simple conversational programs. Never simulate empathy you cannot honor; route to humans for crisis, legal, medical.

   *Source: S2, 13.6.*

   - **What:** Users attribute understanding and intent to even trivially simple conversational programs *(Weizenbaum, 1966)* — vastly amplified by modern LLMs.
   - **Apply:** Empathy display raises disclosure and satisfaction (support bots) — but never simulate empathy you can't honor (crisis, legal, medical); route to humans explicitly when stakes cross the line.

188. **Anthropomorphism (Epley et al., 2007)**

   *Source: S1, line 260.*

   Persona level should match accountability.

   *Source: S2, 13.7.*

   - **What:** People apply social expectations (minds, feelings, accountability) to humanlike agents *(Epley, Waytz & Cacioppo, 2007)*.
   - **Apply:** Humanlike personas raise engagement and tolerance, lower perceived risk of error; the level of anthropomorphism should match accountability: full persona for casual help, plainer interface where errors are costly.

189. **Uncanny valley (Mori, 1970)**

   *Source: S1, line 261.*

   Stay stylized or clearly artificial; disclose bot identity early.

   *Source: S2, 13.8.*

   - **What:** Near-human representations trigger aversion at the "almost but not quite" point *(Mori, 1970)* — applies to avatars, voices, and chat styles.
   - **Apply:** Stay stylized or clearly artificial rather than *almost* human; disclose bot identity early — disclosure + warmth outperforms the trust collapse after discovered deception.

190. **AI disclosure effects (Luo et al., 2019)**

   *Source: S1, line 262.*

   Disclose plainly, pair with capability statements ("drafts in seconds, verify before sending").

   *Source: S2, 13.9.*

   - **What:** Disclosing "this is an AI" shifts perception both ways (warmth/competence tradeoffs); field research (Luo et al., 2019) found chatbot disclosure effects wash out over time, with transparency preserving long-term trust.
   - **Apply:** Disclose plainly and early ("AI assistant — I can make mistakes"); disclosure paired with capability statements ("drafts in seconds, verify before sending") beats both hiding and vague "AI-powered" badges.

191. **Explainability (XAI) and four-lens framework**

   *Source: S1, line 263.*

   Cover model behavior, internal mechanisms, user trust and end-to-end auditability. UI needs: sources and confidence, one-line "why" per output, behavior-level (not model-internal) explanations.

   *Source: S2, 13.10.*

   - **What:** Users need a workable mental model of what the AI does; explanations ("based on your last 30 invoices") fix both trust and misuse.
   - **Apply:** One-line "why" on every AI output, sources on facts, examples of what it can/can't do in empty states; avoid explaining model internals — explain *behavior* in user terms.

192. **Uncertainty communication**

   *Source: S1, line 264.*

   LLM fluency is not evidence. Flag estimates, offer alternatives, ask for context, hedged copy, "low confidence" tags.

   *Source: S2, 13.11.*

   - **What:** LLM output is fluent at every confidence level, so fluency is not evidence; users can't tell confident-wrong from confident-right.
   - **Apply:** Externalize confidence: citations, "low confidence" tags on low-support claims, hedged default copy ("this may be outdated — check"), structured output with checkable parts; visible uncertainty signaling is a top differentiator of trustworthy AI UX in 2025–2026.

193. **Human approval before action**

   *Source: S1, line 265.*

   Un-skippable confirmation for irreversible or consequential agent actions.

194. **Context and memory controls**

   *Source: S1, line 266.*

   Show what history the AI uses; let users inspect, clear and modify it.

195. **Streaming and perceived intelligence**

   *Source: S1, line 267.*

   Stream by default, cancelable "thinking" state, never block the UI, stop button.

   *Source: S2, 13.12.*

   - **What:** Token-streamed responses feel faster and smarter than wait-then-show, even at equal latency; a visible "thinking" state also lowers abandonment.
   - **Apply:** Stream by default, show interim "thinking" with cancelable state, render markdown progressively; never block the UI while generating — partial results + stop button is the current standard.

196. **Co-creation and authorship**

   *Source: S1, line 268.*

   Edited drafts are valued more than accepted ones; keep human edits visible, let users claim authorship.

   *Source: S2, 13.13.*

   - **What:** AI-drafted content raises ownership ambiguity; users value drafts they *edited* more than drafts they *accepted* (IKEA Effect 5.15 transfers to AI).
   - **Apply:** Position AI as draft partner ("edit anything"), keep human edits visible (suggest-changes diff), let users claim authorship in exports; this also raises perceived value of the output.

197. **Conversational UX**

   *Source: S1, line 269.*

   Fast first token, acknowledge before answering, at most one clarifying question, resumable threads, explain silence over about 10 s.

   *Source: S2, 13.15.*

   - **What:** Chat interfaces trigger turn-taking expectations: fast first token, human-paced typing, graceful clarification, conversational error-tolerance norms.
   - **Apply:** Acknowledge before answering ("Looking…"), ask one clarification question max, recover conversationally on failure ("I misread that — did you mean X?"), keep threads resumable; silence over ~10 s must be explained (§11).

198. **Amershi et al. 18 Human-AI guidelines**

   *Source: S1, line 270.*

   Set expectations, contextual help, easy dismissal and correction, graceful handling of uncertainty, explanations, adaptation, notify on material changes.

199. **AI companion dark patterns (CDT)**

   *Source: S1, line 271.*

   Anthropomorphism, synthetic empathy and simulated bonding used to extract data and foster dependency.

200. **Spatial UI, WebXR and Haptics 2.0**

   *Source: S1, line 272.*

   Multisensory feedback intensifies conditioning loops; manipulation moves into physical environments.

201. **Regulation**

   *Source: S1, line 273.*

   EU AI Act (in force Aug 2024; manipulative and subliminal AI prohibitions applicable Feb 2025), FTC AI-overclaim enforcement ("Operation AI Comply", 2024).

   *Source: S2, 13.14.*

   - **What:** Regulators now target AI-specific manipulation: EU AI Act (in force Aug 2024; prohibitions on manipulative/subliminal AI techniques applicable from Feb 2025), FTC enforcement against AI overclaiming ("Operation AI Comply," 2024).
   - **Apply:** Never design AI that exploits emotion at vulnerable moments, fabricates urgency or personas deceptively, or overclaims capability ("100% accurate legal review" = enforcement risk). Compliance is now a design input, not an afterthought.

### Color psychology and color systems

202. **Color-in-context theory (Elliot and Maier)**

   *Source: S1, line 277.*

   Color effects depend on context and combine biological tendencies with learned associations. Red approaches in romance contexts but avoids in achievement contexts (e.g., financial dashboards). The Ecological Valence Theory ties preference to affect toward color-associated objects.

203. **Evidence status**

   *Source: S1, line 278.*

   A systematic review (132 articles, 42,266 participants, 64 countries) found recurring associations: red high-arousal positive and negative; yellow and orange positive high-arousal; blue, green, blue-green, white positive low-arousal; gray negative low-arousal; black negative high-arousal. These are associations, not guaranteed emotion or behavior changes. A 30-nation study (4,598 participants) found shared associations plus variation by language and geography.

204. **Hue, lightness and saturation**

   *Source: S1, line 279.*

   Lightness and saturation often matter as much as hue. Excess saturation can reduce perceived trust and usability.

205. **RGB vs RYB**

   *Source: S1, line 280.*

   Screens mix RGB; pigments RYB.

206. **Personality and saturation tolerance**

   *Source: S1, line 281.*

   The report proposes personality-related preferences; this is not a reliable individual design rule.

207. **Saliency and the unique color singleton**

   *Source: S1, line 282.*

   see Section 5. Color salience is relative.

208. **60-30-10 rule**

   *Source: S1, line 283.*

   60% dominant neutral, 30% secondary, 10% accent reserved for CTAs and critical highlights.

209. **Harmonies**

   *Source: S1, line 284.*

   Monochromatic, analogous (unity, familiarity), complementary (max contrast for key features), split-complementary, triadic, tetradic, neutral plus accent.

210. **Semantic mapping**

   *Source: S1, line 285.*

   - Red: urgency, danger, error, loss; destructive actions, sale, notification badges. Overuse causes anxiety and fatigue; in Chinese contexts red means luck and gain.
     - Blue: trust, calm, stability; primary actions, finance, health. Can feel cold in dating or lifestyle apps.
     - Green: success, growth, go; confirmations, positive portfolio. Loses salience in green-heavy UIs.
     - Yellow: optimism, non-critical attention; warnings. Poor contrast on light backgrounds; large areas fatigue.
     - Orange: energy, action, impulse; buy buttons, upgrades, streaks. Needs isolation to work.
     - Purple: creativity, luxury; needs consistent teaching since conventions are weaker.
     - Black: focus, premium; pure black plus white text causes halation, prefer dark grey.
     - White: clarity, space; needs grouping and hierarchy.
     - Others covered in source summary: teal, cyan, indigo, magenta, pink, brown, beige, cream, gray, metallic gold and silver (details not in the uploaded text).

211. **Button hierarchy by color**

   *Source: S1, line 295.*

   Strong accent for primary (Save), neutral for secondary (Cancel), danger red for destructive (Delete).

212. **Red notification badge as cue**

   *Source: S1, line 296.*

   The report interprets the badge as an urgency cue; bypassing executive control or causing a specific dopamine response is not established by the supplied UI evidence.

213. **Chromatic gamification**

   *Source: S1, line 297.*

   Duolingo streak flame turns grey on loss; Tinder's vivid palette and match feedback.

214. **Retail trading red and green (Robinhood)**

   *Source: S1, line 298.*

   Green validates, red spikes arousal and encourages short-term trading; confetti links trades to game rewards.

215. **Losses disguised as wins (LDWs)**

   *Source: S1, line 299.*

   Multiline slots celebrate a net loss with light and sound. Monash studies (n=940) show overestimated wins and body responses as if won.

216. **Color accessibility (WCAG 2.2)**

   *Source: S1, line 300.*

   Color must not be the sole carrier of meaning; text 4.5:1, large text 3:1, non-text UI 3:1. Pair color with icons, text, patterns.

217. **Color vision deficiency**

   *Source: S1, line 301.*

   Red-green is the most common form; avoid red/green-only status. Prevalence: about 8% of men (document 7), about 2.59% overall in a 2025 meta-analysis (document 5).

218. **APCA**

   *Source: S1, line 302.*

   Perceptual lightness contrast (Lc) accounting for font size, weight and polarity. Example thresholds: Lc 60 regular text and active inputs, 45 large text and key icons, 30 placeholders and focus rings, 15 disabled and structural. Table (size x weight): 14px Lc 100/100/90/75, 16px 90/75/70/60, 18px 75/70/60/55, 24px 60/55/50/45, 36px 45/43/40/38 (Regular/Medium/Semi-bold/Bold). Status: see Section 17.

219. **Dark mode**

   *Source: S1, line 303.*

   Use dark grey over pure black; account for polarity in contrast checks.

220. **OKLCH and CSS Color 4**

   *Source: S1, line 304.*

   oklab() and oklch() give perceptually regular token scales (Candidate Recommendation Draft 2026); still test final pairs.

221. **Cultural variation**

   *Source: S1, line 305.*

   No universal color dictionary.

222. **CTA color myths**

   *Source: S1, line 306.*

   No single conversion-best button color; isolation and contrast matter.

223. **Semantic token architecture and design-system workflow**

   *Source: S1, line 307.*

   Define roles (background, surface, text, primary, accent, success, warning, error) as tokens with light and dark variants.

224. **Humane color interventions**

   *Source: S1, line 308.*

   Replace red badges with grey or pale blue, grayscale modes to lower saliency, equal visual weight on choices, user-customized cues, batched notifications.

225. **Visual engagement is not clinical addiction**

   *Source: S1, line 309.*

   Color effects support attention, association and aesthetic judgment, not chemically caused addiction.

226. **RGB, cone responses and pigment models**

   *Source: S5, prose or supplied summary.*

   The color report discusses display RGB and traditional RYB. Correction: cone sensitivities are overlapping long-, medium-, and short-wavelength responses, rather than literal red/green/blue detector channels; subtractive printing commonly uses CMY/CMYK. RYB is a traditional art model, not a complete pigment-science rule.

227. **Simultaneous contrast**

   *Source: S7, prose or supplied summary.*

   The pasted color summary names simultaneous contrast: surrounding colors affect the appearance of a focal color. Evaluate tokens in their actual adjacent backgrounds and states; isolated swatches are insufficient.

228. **Chromatic adaptation**

   *Source: S7, prose or supplied summary.*

   The pasted color summary names adaptation. Perceived color depends on visual context and adaptation; evaluate an interface across modes and real viewing conditions rather than assuming a HEX value fixes appearance.

229. **Color harmony families**

   *Source: S7, prose or supplied summary.*

   The pasted summary explicitly names monochromatic, analogous, complementary, split-complementary, triadic, tetradic, and neutral-plus-accent systems. They organize palette relationships; harmony does not guarantee accessibility, preference, or engagement.

230. **Palette roles, light/dark variants and contrast validation**

   *Source: S7, prose or supplied summary.*

   The pasted summary reports 40 palettes for productivity SaaS, fintech, health, e-commerce, education, gaming, social products, creator tools, premium products, wellness, analytics, enterprise software, developer tools, messaging, and media. Roles: background, surface, text, primary, accent, success, warning, error. It reports HEX values and contrast checks, but the actual 40 palettes and calculated values were not supplied here and cannot be reconstructed faithfully.

231. **CSS variables, OKLCH scales and designer decision matrices**

   *Source: S7, prose or supplied summary.*

   The pasted color summary names CSS variable examples, an OKLCH token-scale example, a designer decision matrix, and design-system workflows. Retain these implementation topics; the actual code and matrix were not supplied. OKLCH is a color representation, not a psychological principle or accessibility certificate.

232. **Red — color-family coverage**

   *Source: S7, color-family list.*

   Named in the supplied color summary. The source promises representative HEX values, positive/negative associations, UI uses, pairing directions, accessibility problems and evidence labels. Where those details are absent from the uploads, this entry records the coverage without inventing a universal meaning. Consult the semantic-mapping entry for the hues actually explained in the supplied text.

233. **Orange — color-family coverage**

   *Source: S7, color-family list.*

   Named in the supplied color summary. The source promises representative HEX values, positive/negative associations, UI uses, pairing directions, accessibility problems and evidence labels. Where those details are absent from the uploads, this entry records the coverage without inventing a universal meaning. Consult the semantic-mapping entry for the hues actually explained in the supplied text.

### Dark patterns, ethics, regulation and ethical retention

254. **Dark pattern taxonomy**

   *Source: S1, line 313.*

   (Brignull 2010; Gray et al. families: nagging, obstruction, sneaking, interface interference, forced action):
     - Roach motel / hard to cancel / forced continuity.
     - Sneak into basket, hidden costs, drip pricing.
     - Hidden subscription.
     - Preselection (harmful defaults).
     - Visual interference and misdirection (salient preferred button, low-contrast decline).
     - Confirmshaming.
     - Trick wording and trick questions (double negatives, misleading checkbox color).
     - Fake social proof.
     - Fake urgency and scarcity.
     - Nagging.
     - Forced action.
     - Comparison prevention.
     - Disguised ads.
     - Privacy Zuckering.
     - Bait and switch.
     - Currency confusion.
     - AI-specific: fake personas, fabricated AI urgency, overclaimed accuracy, undisclosed synthetic humans, companion emotional manipulation.

255. **Vulnerability each exploits**

   *Source: S1, line 331.*

   sunk cost, present bias, status quo bias, inattentional blindness, cognitive ease, herd mentality, loss aversion, cognitive fatigue, linguistic complexity, information overload.

256. **Hyper-engaging dark patterns (HEDPs) / addictive design**

   *Source: S1, line 332.*

   Platform-wide "habit-forming hypernudges" such as infinite scroll, autoplay, gamification loops outside gaming, interrupting notifications. Linked to anxiety, sleep loss, shorter attention.

257. **Evidence on repeat-use mechanics**

   *Source: S1, line 333.*

   Autoplay has clear causal evidence (2025 study, 76 Netflix users: disabling autoplay cut daily viewing and session length). Infinite scroll evidence is smaller; added friction can interrupt scrolling, varying by self-regulation. Notifications attract attention and interrupt tasks, but disabling them for a week did not reduce screen time in a 2024 trial (it did reduce perceived habit strength and raised FoMO). Recommenders cut search cost but can lower transparency and narrow exposure; diversity is a mitigation.

258. **Dark pattern harm evidence**

   *Source: S1, line 334.*

   A 2026 "transaction test" found dark patterns raised choice inconsistency by about 6 to 12 percentage points; a 2024 review of 642 subscription sites and apps found about 76% had at least one possible dark pattern, about 67% multiple (possible, not all proven unlawful).

259. **Regulation snapshot**

   *Source: S1, line 335.*

   - EU DSA (Art. 25 bans dark patterns on platforms; July 2025 minors guidelines cover addictive behavior; Feb 2026 preliminary finding vs TikTok on addictive design, not a final determination).
     - Proposed EU Digital Fairness Act (reported in source): bans on fake urgency and manipulative defaults, estimated €13.7 billion annual consumer savings, push to treat HEDPs under the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive, limits on personalized ads and dynamic pricing for minors.
     - FTC (ROSCA, click-to-cancel, junk fees, Epic Games settlement 2022 with $245M+ refunds); California CPRA (dark patterns do not constitute consent); UK ICO Age Appropriate Design (no nudging children toward lower privacy, impulsive purchases or excess use).

260. **Intentional friction**

   *Source: S1, line 339.*

   Deliberate pauses (confirmation tap before a large transfer, spatial distancing) force System 2 on high-stakes decisions.

261. **Digital wellbeing and humane design**

   *Source: S1, line 340.*

   Calmer defaults, pause points in feeds, screen-time nudges, internal audits for addictive loops, fair patterns as countermeasures, REBEL framework (e.g., commitment devices to overcome hyperbolic discounting for the user's own goals), Center for Humane Technology "Time Well Spent".

262. **Ethical retention loop**

   *Source: S1, line 341.*

   Need, useful action, visible outcome, optional reminder, return when the need recurs.

263. **Ethical retention rules**

   *Source: S1, line 342.*

   Preserve real stopping cues, easy notification control, exit and cancellation at least as easy as entry (symmetric design), truthful progress, no false scarcity or false social proof, recoverable streaks, explained recommendations, quiet hours, non-personalized alternatives, monitor for involuntary use.

264. **Metrics beyond DAU and session length**

   *Source: S1, line 343.*

   Goal completion, time-to-value, retained skill, post-session satisfaction, regret, failed-stop attempts, late-night minutes, repeated reopenings, notification muting, time after goal completion, self-reported control (a design inference, not a validated universal set).

265. **Designer ethics checklist**

   *Source: S1, line 344.*

   Mirror test (explain it to the user on stage), symmetry test, informed test, grandparent test, regulator test, honest defaults, scarcity and urgency.

266. **Rule of thumb**

   *Source: S1, line 345.*

   Persuasion with truthful premises is design; the same mechanics on fabricated premises is a dark pattern.

267. **Children, age-appropriate controls and wellbeing**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   The supplied summary calls for high-privacy defaults, pause tools, age-appropriate controls and wellbeing-supportive nudges. It warns against nudging children toward extra disclosure, impulsive purchases, or excessive use. Regulatory descriptions remain source reports unless separately verified.

268. **Transaction test and choice inconsistency**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   The summary reports a 2026 experiment in which tested dark patterns increased choice inconsistency by roughly 6–12 percentage points, with larger effects in further market-style tests. Retain the experimental setting and avoid converting this into a universal harm coefficient.

### Applied playbooks

269. **Pricing page**

   *Source: S1, line 381.*

   High anchor, 3 tiers, recommended middle, decoy, annual default with savings framing, social proof under plans, money-back line, full price visibility, one accent CTA. Tripwires: fake "was" prices, hidden seat math, comparison prevention.

   *Source: S2, 15.1.*

   - Anchor high → 3 tiers, recommended middle, decoy per 5.8; annual/monthly toggle with annual default and savings framing (5.4/5.6); social proof under plans (9.3); zero-risk line (5.19: money-back); full price visibility (6.5); one accent CTA (1.19).
   - **Tripwires:** fake "was" prices, hidden seat math, comparison prevention.

270. **Onboarding**

   *Source: S1, line 382.*

   B=MAP audit, one goal question, personalized empty state, honest endowed progress (about 20%), teach one feature by doing, first value in session one, celebrate completion, skip paths. Tripwires: forced tours, email gate before value.

   *Source: S2, 15.2.*

   - B=MAP audit (7.1): one goal question → personalized empty state; progress bar starting ~20% via honest endowed progress (6.9); teach one feature by *doing* (4.4); first-value moment inside session one (5.13); celebrate completion (1.15); skip paths everywhere (1.21).
   - **Tripwires:** forced tour, email gate before any value.

271. **Paywall and trial end**

   *Source: S1, line 383.*

   Show trial artifacts as the user's property, loss-frame concrete features with exact date, offer downgrade before cancel, one-screen respectful cancel flow. Tripwires: cancellation mazes, guilt copy, silent auto-charge.

272. **Notifications and re-engagement**

   *Source: S1, line 384.*

   Routine-anchored cue, valuable variable payload, personal data in message, progressive permission, digest and quiet hours, retire notifications whose open rate flatlines. Tripwires: fake-activity notifications, streak guilt without freezes.

   *Source: S2, 15.4.*

   - Cue anchored to a routine (7.4), payload variable but valuable (7.5), personal data in the message (3.4), progressive permission (10.11), digest + quiet hours built in; kill-or-vary notifications whose open rate flatlines (habituation).
   - **Tripwires:** fake-activity social notifications, streak-guilt without freezes.

273. **Search and results**

   *Source: S1, line 385.*

   Information scent, suggestions, result counts, progressive filters, chunked cards, empty state that teaches a better query, blame-free "did you mean".

   *Source: S2, 15.5.*

   - Information scent in labels (3.12), query suggestions (recognition 1.25), result count + filters that reduce options progressively (1.2), chunked result cards (1.3), empty state teaches a better query, "did you mean" without blame (10.6).

274. **AI feature surfaces**

   *Source: S1, line 386.*

   Disclosure up front, streaming with cancel, uncertainty signals, one-line "why", verification affordances, human escalation for high stakes, capability examples in empty state, no overclaiming.

   *Source: S2, 15.6.*

   - Disclosure up front (13.9); streaming with cancel (13.12); uncertainty signals on facts (13.11); one-line "why" per output (13.10); verification affordances (13.3); human escalation for high stakes (13.6); capability examples in the empty state; overclaim nowhere (13.14).

275. **Forms and settings**

   *Source: S1, line 387.*

   Defaults carry decisions, chunk into steps, validate on blur and forgive format, recognition aids, dangerous settings opt-in with plain consequences, saved progress.

   *Source: S2, 15.7.*

   - Defaults carry the decisions (5.7); chunk into steps (1.3); validate on blur, forgive format (1.16); recognition aids (1.25); dangerous settings opt-in with plain-language consequences; progress saved (1.20).

276. **Errors and empty states**

   *Source: S1, line 388.*

   Blame-free message plus one action, empty states as teachers, skeleton first, recovery over-investment, end every session with a clear next step.

   *Source: S2, 15.8.*

   - Blame-free message + one action (10.6); empty states as teachers with example content and one CTA (4.10); skeleton first (11.3); recovery over-investment (10.7); end every session — even a failed one — with a clear next step (1.15).

277. **Paywall & trial-ending**

   *Source: S2, 15.3.*

   - Show trial artifacts as user property (5.16: "your 14 documents"); loss-frame the concrete features lost (5.5) with the exact date; offer downgrade (not just cancel) as door-in-the-face (9.9); cancel flow = respectful, one screen, saves preferences (10.3), exit survey optional.
   - **Tripwires:** cancellation mazes (§14.12), guilt copy, silent auto-charge.

### Repeat-use mechanics and experimentation

278. **Notifications, interruptions and checking**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   The supplied summary reports performance/strain benefits from fewer interruptions, but a 2024 trial with no reduction in screen time or checking frequency after a week without notifications; perceived habit strength fell and FoMO increased. Preserve both results: notification control supports autonomy without guaranteeing less use.

279. **Infinite scroll and stopping cues**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   Infinite scroll removes natural stopping points and lowers continuation effort. The supplied summary describes a smaller direct experimental literature than public discussion suggests, and a 2026 friction intervention moderated by self-regulation. Preserve pagination, pause points, and user-controlled stopping tools where useful.

280. **Autoplay and continuation automation**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   The summary reports a 2025 experiment involving 76 Netflix users in which disabling autoplay reduced daily viewing and session length. Other reported work links greater automation to inattentiveness and rabbit-hole perceptions. These findings do not establish a universal effect size.

281. **Personalised recommenders, filter bubbles and diversity**

   *Source: S6, prose or supplied summary.*

   Recommendations can reduce search cost while limiting transparency, user control, or exposure diversity. The summary names autonomy, algorithmic awareness, privacy, control, curation, and echo-chamber risks; diversification and non-personalised alternatives are potential mitigations, not guaranteed cures.

### Accessibility and evidence interpretation

282. **Accessibility beyond color and minimum conformance**

   *Source: S7, prose or supplied summary.*

   The pasted summary names perceivability, operability, understandability, robustness, cognitive accessibility, neurodiversity, mobile interaction and cross-cultural design. WCAG does not cover every cognitive or learning-disability need. Conformance criteria, ergonomic recommendations and psychological effects are different kinds of guidance.

283. **Cognitive-digital research heterogeneity and attention control**

   *Source: S7, prose or supplied summary.*

   The pasted summary reports a 2025 review describing heterogeneous, often exploratory cognitive-digital research and a 2026 review arguing attention control can explain complex performance better than simplistic memory-capacity accounts. Treat these as source-reported review conclusions; do not turn them into a universal hierarchy.
